Abstract: Vision-language agents equipped with tools expose intermediate outputs for inspection and comparison with the model's reasoning, which may offer safety benefits over single-shot models. This paper tests that potential benefit when the sensor reading itself is untrusted, so that the tool and reasoning channels share a common visual fault. We consider a dermatology agent based on segmentation, contrastive retrieval, and an iterative vision-language reasoner and define a coupling taxonomy that distinguishes single-channel errors from joint errors that agree or disagree. On PAD-UFES-20, we show that typographic injection produces detectable directional retrieval capture, whereas physical corruption does not. Crop-first retrieval preserves the payload at a readable scale and produces 55% joint wrong agreement across all cases (71% of faulty cases); full-image retrieval produces 35% (43%). Under a binary decision, if both channels are wrong, their predictions must agree, so hard-label channel comparison cannot detect joint failures, limiting disagreement recall to 0.29 (crop-first) versus 0.57 (full-image). The direction of the order effect is reproduced on the independent HIBA test set.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-centric autonomous driving models encode thousands of image tokens per frame, yet most depict sky, buildings, or empty road---content irrelevant to planning. Worse, these tokens dilute the planner's attention: we show that randomly discarding 75% of tokens already outperforms the dense model, because the planner can concentrate on the remaining driving-critical content. Building on this finding, we present Geometry-Aware Token Selection (GATS), which uses LiDAR and radar point projections to select a small fraction of image tokens before the vision backbone runs. Because the selector operates on measured 3D geometry rather than visual features, the backbone encodes only the selected patches, cutting end-to-end FLOPs by up to $18\times$. Each kept token receives a 3D anchor from its LiDAR point and is fed to a self-supervised latent world model that predicts future features and 3D displacements without box annotations. At 25% budget, GATS achieves a 3-sec L2 displacement error of 0.520 m on nuScenes and an EPDMS of 88.9 on NAVSIM---surpassing the dense baseline (0.573 m / 85.9) at $6.6\times$ fewer total FLOPs. At an extreme 10% budget (24 GFLOPs, $18\times$ reduction), it still beats the dense baseline on both nuScenes (0.537 m vs. 0.573 m L2) and NAVSIM (87.9 vs. 85.9 EPDMS). On 739 intent-critical scenarios, GATS covers 95.8% of hazardous vehicles and achieves a 0.09% collision rate---73.5% lower than the dense model (0.34%).

</details>

Abstract: Real-time 4D occupancy forecasting requires reliably extrapolating both ego motion and surrounding actor motion under strict latency budgets. Recent occupancy world models improve long-horizon fidelity through generative decoders and 3D volumetric modeling, yet their computational cost makes real-time deployment challenging. Light-weight BEV forecasters meet latency requirements but often blur or suppress fast-moving actors at longer horizons. We present UFMF (Ultra-Fast Multi-modal Forecaster), a real-time forecaster that keeps an attention-free diagonal state-space temporal core while injecting targeted kinematic and geometric structure where lightweight models typically fail. A radar-guided velocity-aware dynamic prior (VADP) decomposes future displacement into a linear extrapolation plus a learned horizon-dependent residual. The resulting per-cell flow fields transport a compact dynamic latent subspace during rollout. Two auxiliary training constraints complement this branch: world-frame dynamic supervision enforces inter-horizon trajectory coherence, and class-conditioned vertical-profile supervision regularizes BEV-to-3D lifting. UFMF operates at 122 FPS on a single RTX 4090 and reaches 0.45 fast-displacement mIoU on nuScenes, outperforming recent occupancy world models at a fraction of their computational cost.

</details>

Abstract: Long-range detection of small drones is complicated by their visual similarity to birds and by the limited detail available from ground-based cameras. Combining visible and infrared observations offers complementary information, but their usefulness depends on target size, cross-modal alignment, and image quality. We present AVID, a benchmark for studying these factors using real RGB-infrared bird-drone surveillance footage originating from the Drone Detection Dataset and curated for the IEEE ICIP 2025 Video and Image Processing Cup. AVID comprises 32,040 corresponding frame pairs from 115 videos, with 35,952 existing bird and drone annotations at $320\times256$ resolution. Targets occupy an unusually narrow size range: 68.1\% have a geometric-mean bounding-box side of 8--16 pixels, and 99.0\% meet the COCO small-object criterion. To characterize the geometric difficulty of combining the modalities, we introduce an object-relative alignment ratio and an alignment burden curve. In a sample of 300 frame pairs, 32.3\% have estimated global translations greater than 5 pixels; among the 264 pairs with matching annotations, 16.3\% exceed one median target side. We further introduce AVID-C, which simulates seven families of image degradation at five severity levels independently in RGB, infrared, or both. A fixed-feature analysis examines how scale, degradation, and added cross-modal shifts affect fitted bird-drone class overlap. At 12--16 pixels, the Bhattacharyya error bounds are 0.148 for RGB, 0.198 for infrared, and 0.046 for their combined features. These values describe separation in the specified feature and density models, not detector accuracy. Together, AVID and AVID-C support scale-aware, alignment-aware, and sensor-specific studies of tiny aerial targets.

</details>

Abstract: We present FieldGuideRAG, a training-free GraphRAG method in which a vision-language model, or VLM, recognizes fine-grained species by comparing the query with real photographs stored on the nodes of a knowledge graph. Multimodal GraphRAG systems describe each entity in text, and text conveys little of the visual detail that separates look-alike species. Our graph stores one photograph on every species node and links each species to its relatives, its geographic range and its ecological facts. A frozen VLM verifies the query against the photograph of each candidate node. FieldGuideRAG fuses the verification scores with the range edge and answers questions about the organism from the fact edges of the verified species. On CUB-200, a Wikidata graph and four iNaturalist domains, the node photograph improves verification over the species name on every graph, while text descriptions add little. Verification of the candidate pool exceeds a SigLIP retriever by up to 16 points, the range edge adds 7 to 19 points on iNaturalist, and answering from fact edges is correct on up to 89.5% of 28,820 ecological questions. The verification scores also detect queries whose species is missing from the graph. Graphs, prompts and code will be released.

</details>

Abstract: Recent diffusion-based methods have substantially advanced image editing, yet parametric 3D control at the part level remains challenging. Existing approaches either operate on whole objects with manual rigging, or revert to 2D handles at the part level. They also struggle to model appearance and texture under large transformations, leading to unrealistic outputs in geometry-intensive edits. To address these limitations, we introduce RADI3N, a framework for semantically-grounded part-aware object manipulation with parametric 3D control. Our CS-ARAP deformation module suppresses rotation-field discontinuities at part boundaries via neighborhood covariance smoothing, producing structurally consistent deformations under complex part-level edits. We further introduce an inversion-free selective texture transfer mechanism that suppresses high-frequency positional biases and scales up low-frequency semantic cues within cross-image attention. This eliminates strict spatial bias and supports semantic matching, allowing source appearance to follow the edited part onto its new geometry. Experiments demonstrate that RADI3N achieves state-of-the-art performance in geometric fidelity, viewpoint consistency, and controllability.

</details>

Abstract: Single-exposure high dynamic range (HDR) novel view synthesis (NVS) recovers HDR scenes from low dynamic range (LDR) views captured at a single exposure and estimates camera response function (CRF) for LDR re-exposure. Without HDR ground-truth, using single-exposure LDR views as the sole source of direct supervision underconstrains HDR reconstruction and cannot ensure its faithful recovery, motivating the need for learned LDR-to-HDR reconstruction priors. Moreover, single-exposure LDR views supervise CRF only over a limited range, necessitating a learnable prior for reliable re-exposure beyond it. We propose PriorGS, a 3D Gaussian Splatting framework that addresses both challenges by (1) guiding HDR recovery with pseudo-HDR supervision from 2D single-exposure HDR reconstruction methods while anchoring it to input LDR views through a closed reconstruction loop; and (2) regularizing CRF modeling with a learnable prior that guarantees monotonicity and promotes plausible responses. Yet cross-view inconsistencies in independently generated pseudo-HDR targets impair scene reconstruction when HDR is encoded directly as Gaussian's color attribute. We therefore introduce a 3D base-gain HDR representation that factors HDR radiance into an LDR base and an overlaid 3D gain field, promoting robustness to such inconsistencies and enabling 3D HDR appearance editing. Extensive experiments demonstrate PriorGS's substantial superiority over baselines.

</details>

Abstract: Web overlays, such as cookie consent banners, newsletter prompts, and age gates, obstruct automated web crawlers and degrade downstream document representations. While proprietary multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can accurately identify overlays, their latency and inference cost preclude web-scale ingestion. Conversely, lightweight vision models struggle with full-page renderings characterized by extreme vertical aspect ratios, where standard isotropic resizing collapses critical text features and causes false positives. In this work, we present WebOverlay, a high-throughput vision-only detector trained via distillation from a frontier vision-language teacher. We establish a dynamic tiling strategy that partitions extreme aspect-ratio web renders into aspect-preserving patches with spatial overlap, coupled with an aspect-ratio-aware boundary adjudication mechanism to eliminate boundary artifacts. To eliminate cross-modal latency overheads, our student model relies purely on visual cues, bypassing DOM tree parsing. WebOverlay significantly outperforms DOM-based heuristics and achieves competitive precision and recall against heavy proprietary MLLM baselines while delivering multiple orders of magnitude higher throughput and substantially reduced deployment costs.

</details>

Abstract: Robot perception is increasingly data driven, yet field data is slow, costly, and sometimes disruptive to collect. Video world models offer a cheaper source: seeded with real frames, they extend a recorded scene into new footage with the deployment's sensor, altitude, and environment. We ask whether such synthetic data matches the utility of real field data and whether detectors trained with it perform in the field. Seeding a 7B-parameter world model with wildlife drone video, we more than double the training set with curated frames. On held-out videos from the same drones, mAP@50--95 rises 4.3\% when these frames are added to real data and 10.3\% when they replace it, and up to 33.5\% on an urban benchmark. Although an expert rejects an estimated 87.5\% of generated clips, the data costs about \$1,100 of GPU time, against more than \$3,200 of field labor alone for a less useful real training set. Synthetic data thus shifts data costs from fieldwork to compute.

</details>

Abstract: Background: Limited tumor annotations and incomplete labels across MRI datasets hinder unified segmentation of pancreatic tumors and organs at risk (OARs) for MRI-guided radiotherapy. Purpose: We propose Pancreas-MedNeXt, a unified model that segments the whole pancreas, its subregions, tumors, and surrounding OARs from one MRI volume. Methods: The model uses a shared MedNeXt encoder-decoder with supervised whole-pancreas and subregion pretraining, followed by multitask fine-tuning. Three output heads predict the pancreatic subregions, tumor, and OARs (stomach, duodenum, kidneys). An annotation-aware loss allows training on partially labeled data without treating unannotated structures as background. Pancreas and tumor were evaluated by three-fold cross-validation on 142 PANTHER patients (92 diagnostic, 50 MR-Linac), subregions and OARs on Cyst-X and AMOS test sets, and pancreas and OARs on 33 external T1-weighted TotalSegmentator scans. Comparisons with nnU-Net, MedNeXt, VISTA3D, and Swin UNETR used the Dice similarity coefficient (DSC) with paired bootstrap 95% CIs. Results: Pancreas-MedNeXt achieved the highest mean pancreas and tumor DSC on diagnostic and MR-Linac MRI. Tumor DSC was 58.41% and 42.55%, exceeding task-specific MedNeXt by 4.94 (95% CI: 0.28-9.45) and 5.89 (95% CI: 2.37-9.72) percentage points, respectively. Whole-pancreas DSC was 85.73% and 70.66%. Pretraining improved head, body, and tail DSC by 2.01, 3.00, and 3.78 percentage points over training from scratch. It also achieved the highest DSC for the pancreas, duodenum, and right kidney on AMOS, and for all five organs on external MRI, exceeding the next-best method by 4.92-8.85 percentage points.Conclusion: A single Pancreas-MedNeXt model segments tumors, pancreatic subregions, and OARs on diagnostic and MR-Linac MRI, which could streamline contouring for pancreatic cancer radiotherapy.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-rater segmentation datasets show that a single image can admit several valid masks, yet most training sets expose only one annotation per image. We study single-annotation multi-hypothesis segmentation: each training image exposes one fixed mask, while the model must return a finite bank that covers held-out human annotations at test time. Our experiments reveal a representation tradeoff. Banks learned directly from images recover variations in local boundaries and extent but lose structural hypotheses, whereas frozen semantic features preserve structural grouping but underrepresent spatial variation. We introduce the Semantic-Spatial Residual Bank (SSRB), which freezes a semantic bank trained with balanced assignments and learns a residual from the image for each hypothesis. Across five medical endpoints, SSRB improves coverage of held-out masks at $K=8$ over a Probabilistic U-Net under the same one-mask constraint. On LIDC and LIDC-hard, it also produces substantially more distinct useful masks. On BSDS, it preserves the semantic bank's performance in grouping regions. Its advantage grows as more masks are returned, showing that residuals learned for corresponding heads improve coverage of local annotation variation while preserving how regions are grouped.

</details>

Abstract: Image composition labels such as center, diagonal, or rule of thirds describe visual structure rather than object categories. KU-PCP provides image-level labels and class-specific geometric elements, but does not show which visible regions people associate with each of its nine classes. We introduce KU-PCP Elements (KU-PCPE), a label-conditioned region-evidence benchmark for photographic composition. Given an image and a label already judged applicable, the task is to segment the visible regions that explain why the label applies. KU-PCPE contains 4,093 single-annotator training masks and a 1,327-task expert test split with three masks per image-label task. The annotations also include exploratory segment-versus-line ratings that describe whether region or semantic-guideline evidence better explains the label. A held-out expert reaches .867 Dice against the other experts, while the strongest deployable model reaches .664, a proposal oracle .672, and the best vision-language model (VLM) proposal selector .395. KU-PCPE exposes the gap between recognizing a composition label and grounding the human-aligned visual evidence that makes it plausible in a particular image.

</details>

Abstract: Radiologists' visual attention can supervise medical image learning, but how reader panel composition affects its value remains untested. We evaluated GazeRIB (Gaze-guided Radiologist-Informed Blackout), which masks unviewed regions during training and uses unmasked images at inference. Eight experts repeatedly read the same 80 chest X-rays (CXRs). We compared individual readers with three-, five- and eight-reader panels across seven architectures and 10 seeds, evaluating 910 models on a separate 2,897-CXR cohort for four-class severity prediction. Every panel improved macro F1 over the no-gaze baseline by 5.33-7.47 percentage points (pp; Bonferroni-adjusted p < 0.007); no individual reader reached significance on this endpoint. Three readers captured 73-80% of the eight-reader gain in macro F1, the area under the receiver-operating-characteristic curve (ROC-AUC) and the area under the precision-recall curve (AU-PRC). On external validation without retraining, all four panels improved ROC-AUC on SIIM-FISABIO-RSNA by +1.93 to +4.24 pp (p <= 0.004); on MIMIC-Eye no panel differed significantly from baseline, whereas the single-reader average declined (-1.40 pp, p = 0.008). Reader composition therefore influenced performance, although transfer depended on the cohort.

</details>

Abstract: Rooftop solar potential estimation often relies on LiDAR, digital surface models (DSMs), or pipelines that explicitly reconstruct roof geometry before estimating solar potential. We propose Direct Rooftop Solar Prediction (DRSP), an end-to-end formulation that maps satellite imagery and coarse regional sunlight information directly to application-level solar outputs. DRSP neither requires explicit geometric representations as input nor predicts roof orientation, slope, height, DSMs, or other geometric quantities as intermediate outputs, and therefore requires no geometric intermediate supervision. To enable large-scale study of this formulation, we develop a programmatic data-curation pipeline that converts per-panel information from the Google Solar API into usable-rooftop masks and spatial solar-energy targets, yielding two geographically distinct datasets containing approximately 60K rooftops from the U.S. Midwest and 87K from Texas. We demonstrate the feasibility of DRSP and systematically characterize the effects of geographic training distribution, model depth, image resolution, regional sunlight conditioning, and heatmap regression objectives. Overall, our results suggest that useful rooftop-scale solar information can be learned directly from 2D imagery without explicit geometric intermediate representations, offering a simpler path toward large-scale solar assessment. Our code and dataset are available at https://github.com/joshualee124/direct_rooftop_solar_prediction.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language compositionality is the ability to identify which properties, relations, quantities, and states bind to which entities in both an image and a caption, and to match those bindings across modalities. Existing benchmarks evaluate this ability through image-text matching: a model ranks candidate image-caption match similarities and succeeds when the correct match comes on top. In such datasets, paired images differ by more than a single binding: scene, layout, and content all differ, with 97% of pixels changing on average. Such pairs do not isolate compositionality: results are confounded by global image differences. We introduce Compositional Single-EdiT (CompoSET), a benchmark dataset in which each base image-caption pair has an edited counterpart, or foil, that preserves the scene but differs by a single controlled edit, such as color, count, spatial relation, or object state. Base and foil images preserve naturalistic scene complexity yet are visually near-identical: only 8% of pixels change on average. Our dataset has many foils per base scene (~22) in order to separate scene difficulty from edit difficulty, a distinction one-foil-per-base benchmarks cannot make. CompoSET contains 1,776 instances across 80 naturalistic scenes, spanning 16 edit types in 4 semantic categories, with captions at 3 verbosity levels. We test 22 dual-encoder VLMs and find that all models perform at or below chance level on CompoSET, despite scoring well on prior benchmarks. This suggests that apparent progress under prior image-text matching protocols may be an artifact arising from confounds in benchmark design, and that compositional understanding in dual-encoder VLMs is far from solved. CompoSET addresses the confounds and offers a better controlled benchmark to measure future progress.

</details>


### [121] [MotorSense: A Video-EMG Dataset of Motoric Representations for Action Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41127)
*Eadom Dessalene,Michael Maynord,Amir-Hossein Shahidzadeh,Botao He,Yasamin Tabatabaee,Ruoshi Liu,Ruohan Gao,Paul Pu Liang,Yiannis Aloimonos*

Abstract: Computer vision models learn hand action only from its visual consequences, without access to the underlying embodied motor signals. We address this absence with MotorSense, the first large-scale paired egocentric video and surface electromyography (EMG) dataset. MotorSense pairs bimanual EMG bands with egocentric video and yields synchronized video, bimanual EMG and IMU, and audio across 100 hours of unscripted manipulation. EMG offers a powerful scalable alternative to manual annotation: it captures finger movement, hand configuration, and the forces exerted between hand and environment as a dense signal free of occlusion and labeler bias, whereas manual annotation is costly, subjective, and unreliable under occlusion. We use MotorSense to learn sensorimotor alignment, producing an organization of action that is shared across the visual and motoric spaces, through cross-modal pre-training between video and EMG. The resultant vision models are then transferred to downstream tasks that benefit from sensorimotor alignment: in this case, action recognition, 3D hand reconstruction, and 3D contact reconstruction. We further demonstrate a generative application in which EMG motor signals are used to condition a video diffusion model, yielding videos of fine-grained hand manipulation. Across four action-centric computer vision tasks and eight popular video datasets, we demonstrate that sensorimotor alignment provides a stronger foundation for visual representation learning than conventional self-supervised learning methods over video, outperforming every baseline across all tasks and datasets evaluated.

</details>

Abstract: Synthesizing realistic tumors on medical images helps address data scarcity for clinical AI. Existing diffusion-based methods, however, lack fine-grained attribute-level controllability, closed-loop quality verification, and awareness of which morphological patterns are underrepresented in the target distribution. We present MATS, a multi-agent framework for text-controllable, cross-organ tumor synthesis. Given a target disease, MATS first profiles its morphological distribution over a fine-grained attribute space covering tumor geometry and appearance, and constructs a generation plan that amplifies underrepresented subtypes while keeping common ones dominant. It then localizes the target organ, proposes anatomically valid placement, and executes two-stage diffusion to produce the tumor mask and render texture, each conditioned on the corresponding attributes. A closed-loop reflective mechanism verifies attribute alignment at three checkpoints throughout synthesis and provides targeted feedback to guide re-generation when misalignment is detected. Trained exclusively on a single-source pancreatic neuroendocrine tumor dataset, MATS consistently improves downstream segmentation both in-domain and across LiTS, KiTS, and MSD-Pancreas benchmarks, whereas most prior generative augmentation methods fall below real-only training.

</details>

Abstract: Monocular depth estimation is a fundamental capability for 3D spatial perception in robotic and intelligent systems. While data-driven discriminative methods have achieved compelling performance for monocular depth estimation, generative approaches can reconstruct fine structural details with stronger generalization. However, most existing generative diffusion models suffer from low inference efficiency caused by multi-step sampling, whereas single-step diffusion models struggle to recover high-fidelity depth maps. To mitigate these drawbacks, this paper presents FlowMoDE, a coarse-to-fine flow-matching-based framework for efficient, structure-aware monocular depth estimation supporting sim-to-real and cross-scene generalization. The core architecture first conducts coarse-level decoding conditioned on diffused features extracted from a pre-trained semantic encoder, and subsequently restores fine-grained structural patterns via expanded decoding layers. Trained exclusively on a single synthetic indoor dataset, our proposed method outperforms prior methods across five real-world indoor and outdoor datasets, yielding an average 14.1% reduction in relative error against state-of-the-art works as well as 9.9\% reduction in chamfer distance for point cloud reconstruction. We further validate the generalization capability of FlowMoDE on realistic robotic scenes, with qualitative comparisons against state-of-the-art methods. The project page is https://flowmode-shao.netlify.app/

</details>


### [124] [Beyond CLIP: Calibrated (Few-Shot) Defect Detection With System One Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41199)

Abstract: Cameras and vision sensors are widely used for product and process inspection in manufacturing environments. Traditionally, this process required thousands of images of both nominal and defective products to train such models. Recent research shows that vision transformer (ViT) models, such as CLIP, can reduce this requirement to 50-100 images per nominal and defective class. However, collecting even 50 defective images can be difficult in high-yield or low-volume manufacturing processes. In this paper, we examine the utility of two recently proposed structured decision models (Jev and Clef-Flash) in applications using as few as k=2 reference images per class at inference. In the process, we provide three main contributions: (a) a framework for k-shot learning of these models for image classification/inspection applications; (b) an agentic approach for automatically refining the text descriptions that guide image classification in these decision models; and (c) an approach to calibrate the resulting defect probabilities to improve their reliability for inspection decisions. We test these contributions in five manufacturing case studies. Using two reference images per class, both Jev and Clef-Flash outperform CLIP's test accuracies with the same references (0.75-1.00 [Jev] and 0.71-0.99 [Clef-Flash] vs 0.67-0.84 [CLIP]). Furthermore, we show that our agentic refinement of text descriptions significantly improves accuracy across 6 of 8 model-application combinations, and our calibration approach reduces the Brier score in 13 of the 15 model-application configurations. To support the reproducibility of our work, we provide our replication code and have created the R "decisions" package for evaluating additional models. We also provide a publicly available web app that allows practitioners to inspect their own images with no code.

</details>

Abstract: RGB-thermal (RGB-T) object detectors combine visible and thermal imagery for robust detection, but signal corruption in either stream can substantially degrade accuracy. Restoration-based methods incur significant latency and do not consistently improve downstream accuracy, raising a more direct question: rather than restoring corrupted inputs, can we efficiently adapt the detector to their varying signal quality? Conventional PEFT offers an efficient route, but its input-independent corrections cannot track diverse test-time corruptions. Our controlled corruption sweeps show that corruption types have similar effects near clean conditions but diverge increasingly with severity, motivating corruption-aware, per-input adaptation. We therefore propose PG-FM (Prompt-Guided Feature Modulation), which adapts RGB-T object detectors to the estimated corruption type and severity of each modality. Under controlled RGB-T corruption simulations, PG-FM preserves clean-set performance, improves mAP50 by up to 20.8 points on FLIR and 19.9 points on KAIST, and transfers without retraining to unmodified, naturally acquired external RGB-T datasets.

</details>

Abstract: Real-world image editing requires coordinating multiple visual transformations with content preservation and reference constraints, yet aggregate scores can readily obscure unmet local requirements. Effective evaluation must therefore identify local failures and translate them into priorities for subsequent model improvement. We introduce WeGenEditBench, comprising 2,740 evaluation instances and 148 task types across single-image, text-intensive, and multi-reference editing. Pre-generated checklists shared across models decompose each request into atomic requirements; item-level deductions and visual evidence are established first and then summarized into scores for edit execution, preservation, visual quality, and task-specific correctness. To support the experience-acquisition component of recursive self-improvement (RSI), we develop WeGenEvoStudio, a unified multi-agent harness for RSI-oriented learning that integrates model-conditioned failure diagnosis, retrieval-grounded data construction, supervised adaptation, and re-evaluation. Among 15 evaluated configurations, GPT-Image-2 leads single-image editing with an Overall score of 4.076, while Seedream-5.0-Pro leads text-intensive editing at 4.291. Evolution experiments across four generation and editing benchmarks demonstrate substantial targeted gains: on their report-selected Top-5 single-image targets, FLUX.2 [dev] improves Overall from 1.083 to 3.517, while Qwen-Image-Edit-2511 gains 13.2%. Together, our benchmark and harness provide a foundation for developing more reliable image models through interpretable evaluation and feedback-driven learning.

</details>

Abstract: Coarse-to-fine 3D-native generation relies heavily on the first-stage sparse structure, which determines the global orientation and overall geometry for subsequent refinement. However, existing coarse generators suffer from two key limitations: their orientation is difficult to control due to ambiguous semantic canonicalization, and their structures are often inconsistent with input images due to weak 2D-3D grounding. We propose Coarse3D, a sparse-structure generation model that produces orientation-controllable and structurally faithful voxel geometry from arbitrary unposed images. Coarse3D introduces three designs: a first-frame anchored canonical space that aligns generation with the first input view while preserving object-centric normalization; a dual-tower DiT that couples generation with auxiliary feed-forward reconstruction to learn spatially grounded features; and spatial-prior guided attention that regularizes cross-attention with pixel-to-voxel correspondence. Coarse3D can directly replace the coarse stage of existing pipelines. Experiments show that it substantially improves sparse structure quality and consistently enhances final 3D generation, achieving state-of-the-art performance.

</details>

Abstract: While modern Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have been remarkably successful in general scene text recognition, they consistently struggle with the long-tail distribution of text that is degraded by motion blur or low resolution. These limitations are particularly apparent in egocentric applications like smart glasses, where text is often captured in noisy environments. In this paper, we introduce the \textbf{BSTRB} (Blurry Scene Text Recognition Benchmark), a novel benchmark of 8,398 noisy image samples with high-fidelity annotations derived from 1,699 real-world video sequences. To support this, we present \textbf{Hawkeye}, a VLM fine-tuned on BSTRB to read and reason about illegible text by leveraging contextual priors and pixel-level alignment. Our results show that \name matches much larger VLMs on BSTRB overall and exceeds them on text at or below 20 pixels, in a single pass suited to real-time deployment. We characterize the resolution at which current VLMs begin to fail.

</details>

Abstract: Reasoning video object segmentation (ReVOS) requires segmenting the target specified by an implicit query throughout a video. Existing MLLM-based methods typically require costly cross-model alignment, tying MLLM training to a downstream segmentation model. We present ReVEAL, a decoupled thinking-with-video framework for ReVOS that elicits role-aware latent visual evidence from MLLM reasoning to directly guide segmentation. ReVEAL introduces Visual Evidence Tokens (VETs) into video reasoning to associate necessary visual regions or entities with target, background, or distractor roles, while a Dynamic Attention Layer Router adaptively aggregates VET-to-video attention across layers into role-specific grounding evidence. We train the MLLM to perform VET-augmented reasoning on our constructed ReVEAL-47K dataset, with a Contrastive Attention Grounding Loss as auxiliary supervision to apply role-specific constraints to VET attention and optimize layer routing for reliable latent grounding. At inference, ReVEAL identifies the target through this thinking-with-video process and fuses the resulting role-specific evidence to guide a frozen SAM2 in predicting target masks. Experiments on five benchmarks show that ReVEAL outperforms alignment-free baselines and remains competitive with alignment-based methods.

</details>

Abstract: Existing sketch generation methods often rely on image guidance and are largely limited to individual objects or simplified scenes, leaving text-guided complex scene sketching underexplored. We present SketchDirector, an agentic framework that mimics the human sketching workflow for automatic multi-concept scene sketching. Given a scene-level text prompt, SketchDirector first employs a large language model~(LLM) to decompose the scene into multiple draft assets, where each is represented by a set of Bézier curves. Then, an agentic reasoning loop iteratively plans and executes their spatial arrangement, guided by self-evolving memory. Finally, a detail refinement module leverages diffusion priors to enhance sketch quality while preserving geometric structure. To support future research and applications in multi-concept scene sketch generation, we further introduce the \textit{MCSketch8K} dataset, comprising editable vector assets, compositional layouts, and object-level manipulation annotations. Extensive experiments show that SketchDirector outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in semantic and compositional quality while effectively preserving overall contour organization during refinement. The project page is: \href{https://yiki77.github.io/SketchDirector/}{https://yiki77.github.io/SketchDirector/}.

</details>

Abstract: Variable interaction distances continuously degrade facial resolution in edge-based affective interaction, making low-resolution facial expression recognition (FER) difficult under limited computation. Full-face image or feature restoration incurs substantial overhead, whereas lightweight recognition pipelines may miss local cues that remain discriminative after degradation. We propose STAR-FER, a scale-and-task-adaptive selective restoration framework that balances recognition performance and resource constraints. A lightweight image-scale conditioned Gate jointly predicts a pixel-wise spatial ranking and a continuous restoration budget, then applies exact-support projection to obtain an input-dependent free-form mask. The mask-conditioned restorer retains complete facial context while concentrating sparse restoration and residual insertion on the selected support. Across seven degradation scales from x2 to x8 on the complete RAF-DB test set, STAR-FER achieves 80.92% mean accuracy and 71.64% Macro-F1, improving the low-resolution ResNet-18 baseline by 2.25 and 2.71 percentage points, respectively. It surpasses SR-FER by 1.04 accuracy points while requiring 8.84 GFLOPs, a 54.29% reduction, with 28.01% mean mask coverage, and maintains effective recognition at unseen non-integer scales. These results show that spatial-ranking and continuous-budget driven selective restoration reduces spatial modification and computation while maintaining low-resolution FER performance, offering an efficient path to resource-constrained affective interaction.

</details>

Abstract: Dynamic spatial reasoning is essential for connecting visual intelligence to thephysical world, yet remains challenging for vision-language models (VLMs), which must preserve scene state while tracking how spatial relations evolve over time. Meanwhile, video generation models (VGMs), trained to synthesize temporally coherent world dynamics, offer a rich source of spatiotemporal representations. We present GenDSR, a framework that transfers intermediate representations from VGM into VLM for dynamic spatial reasoning. Rather than treating a VGM feature as a monolithic prior, GenDSR constructs two complementary branches: a raw branch preserving holistic generative context and a delta branch emphasizing temporal variation. Task-conditioned tokenwise gates then fuse both signals and inject the result into the native visual representation. On DSR-Bench, GenDSR achieves 67.2%, surpassing the previous best result and establishing a new state of the art.

</details>

Abstract: Pre-trained models offer complementary strengths in CT organ segmentation, but their relative accuracy varies across organs and spatial regions. Multi-teacher knowledge distillation can combine these strengths in a single student, yet global or instance-level teacher weights overlook variation within a 3D volume. We propose Task-Performance Routing (TPR), which uses region-wise segmentation errors to determine teacher weights during training without learned routing parameters. In hard regions, TPR favors teachers with lower prediction errors; in easy regions, it uses uniform weights to combine their knowledge. A hierarchical spatial partition provides fine-grained routing in shallow encoder stages and coarse-grained routing in deep stages. Experiments on BTCV, MSD-Liver, and MSD-Pancreas with two teacher pairs and two backbone architectures show higher Dice scores than the individual teachers, adaptive ensembles, and evaluated multi-teacher distillation baselines. On MSD-Pancreas, TPR achieves Dice scores of 65.40% with SwinUNETR and 68.25% with U-Net, compared with 64.38% and 67.45% for the corresponding adaptive ensembles. Inference uses the student alone, with no routing or teacher overhead. Code is available at https://github.com/EdvinCecilia/TPR_MultiTeacher.

</details>

Abstract: We present Queryable Gaussian View Index (QGVI) for querying existing Gaussian scene assets and their associated calibrated reference data. Text-view retrieval and selective RGB camera localization return poses in the asset's coordinate frame. QGVI-T turns Gaussian-rendered views into a language-searchable index of scene locations, binding structured captions to known camera poses and place identifiers. Semantic and lexical retrieval select views from distinct places without online rendering, VLM calls, or a scene-specific pose network. The image route, QGVI-I, filters LoGS-based geometric estimates using distinct inlier query keypoints. It omits photometric refinement and preserves accepted pose coordinates. On nine development scenes, QGVI-T achieves 58.3% Top-1 and 78.3% Top-5 joint relevance-and-validity success, compared with 26.1% and 34.6% for our CityLoC reimplementation with refinement. QGVI-I achieves 97.3% scene-mean pose success at 1 m/2 degrees, with pooled false-acceptance and false-rejection rates of 0.707% and 1.142%. A paired cached evaluation retains all 1,775 initializer poses correct at this threshold. Recorded median text query latency is 0.020 s. Matched controls show competitive BM25 retrieval and slightly lower false acceptance for thresholded full LoGS; grid augmentation suggests modest gains on novel-view-derived descriptions. We report model-judged semantic success separately from source-camera recovery, with source-verified or estimated scales for geometric evaluation.

</details>


### [135] [Parse Before You Execute: Efficient Spatial Reasoning with Executable Primitives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41389)
*Xiwei Liu,Yulong Li,Xinling Zhuang,Xuhui Li,Yichen Li,Haolin Yang,Zhixiang Lu,Yutong Xie*

Abstract: Spatial reasoning in Vision-Language Models (VLMs) has increasingly benefited from tool-integrated pipelines that ground computation in explicit geometry. However, existing systems often compile queries into relatively open-ended artifacts, such as code, task constraints, tool plans, or gathered evidence, leaving what must be resolved before execution under-specified. This can lead to unnecessary tool acquisition and iterative refinement, while still producing plausible reasoning traces from incomplete spatial commitments. We identify this failure mode as under-committed query compilation. To address it, we propose PEPS, a training-free agentic paradigm Parsing Executable Primitives for Spatial reasoning under a typed FESM schema over Frame, Entity, State, and Metric. PEPS follows a parse-before-execute principle: the Parser commits each query to a compact set of executable requirements; the Executor selectively grounds the required primitives through fixed geometric APIs and deterministic computation; and the Verifier checks execution sufficiency, enabling early termination or targeted repair. Extensive experiments across challenging spatial reasoning benchmarks show that PEPS consistently improves reasoning accuracy while substantially reducing refinement rounds, API cost, and end-to-end latency over strong tool-integrated baselines. These results demonstrate that explicit requirement commitment can make tool-integrated spatial reasoning not only more reliable, but also more execution-efficient.

</details>


### [136] [DUGO: Dual-Scale Gaussian Memory for Online Temporal Action Localization in Egocentric Procedural Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41402)
*Mohammed Aman Bhuiyan,Md. Sazzad Hossain Adib,Samiul Basir Bhuiyan,Mir Faiyaz Hossain,Syed Mohammed Shamsul Islam,Md Zakir Hossain,Shafin Rahman*

Abstract: Online Temporal Action Localization (OnTAL) aims to detect and localize action instances from untrimmed streaming videos in real time. Unlike offline approaches, OnTAL models have no access to future frames and cannot go back and refine earlier predictions, which makes the task considerably harder given the strict real-time constraints and narrow temporal window available. Most existing OnTAL methods lean on either brief anchor-based context or a single-scale memory of past frames, which limits how well they can capture actions unfolding across multiple time scales or requiring long-range reasoning in Procedural Videos. To address these gaps, we present DUGO (Dual-Scale Encoding and Gaussian Memory Online), a new OnTAL framework that jointly models local motion dynamics and long-range temporal dependencies. At its core, DUGO uses a Dual-Scale Temporal Encoder, pairing depth-wise local representations with global self-attention so it can pick up both fine-grained temporal patterns and broader contextual cues. We also introduce a Gaussian Memory Bank, which represents action memory as stochastic, input-conditioned Gaussian prototypes, allowing for richer temporal memory through gated cross-attention applied at both the encoder and anchor-query stages. Extensive experiments on procedural egocentric datasets (EGTEA, EPIC-Kitchens-100) demonstrate that DUGO achieves state-of-the-art performance while also providing substantial improvements on non-egocentric benchmarks such as Muses. The project page is available at: https://aman554-eq.github.io/DUGO

</details>


### [137] [SACQ: Structure-Aware Codebook Quantization for Ultra-Low-Bit Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41408)

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models enable capable robot behavior, but their large memory footprint limits deployment on resource-constrained robotic platforms. Weight quantization is a natural remedy, yet post-training quantization (PTQ) recipes inherited from large language models degrade when applied to VLAs at ultra-low precision. Existing low-bit PTQ methods often fail to explicitly account for the structure of downstream operations and allocate precision without considering its effect on the policy actions. We introduce SACQ, an ultra- low-bit weight-only vector-codebook quantization framework for VLAs. SACQ combines (1) structure-aware codebook fitting with role-specific quadratic metrics that reflect input statistics and downstream sensitivity; (2) action-aware mixed-bit allocation based on the increase in final-action error as individual layers are compressed; and (3) two-stage post-quantization refinement that reassigns codeword indices with second-order error compensation and calibrates codebooks and row-group scales against the decoded action. Using GR00T N1.7, SACQ achieves 93.85% average success across four LIBERO suites at 1.5 bits per language/action weight while reducing model memory by 4.83x. With INT8 activation quantization and low-precision attention, it retains 93.55% success while running 1.51x faster than BF16. On SimplER, SACQ further achieves 63.76% and 52.27% success under Visual Matching and Variant Aggregation at 2 bits per language/action weight, compared to 58.90% and 53.45% for BF16, respectively.

</details>

Abstract: Existing Scene Text Recognition (STR) models are inherently coupled with a specific decoding strategy by their training paradigms, limiting their flexibility in deployment across diverse recognition scenarios. In this paper, we propose FlexReader, an STR model that supports multiple decoding strides, enabling a single model to achieve an optimal accuracy-efficiency trade-off across different datasets. Specifically, we first introduce a Sub-string Perception module to perceive the target sub-string from visual features, followed by multiple parallel decoding blocks that independently decode each character in the sub-string. During inference, FlexReader supports decoding with arbitrary strides within the perceived sub-string range. Furthermore, we propose Adaptive Prefix Decoding, which adaptively selects the decoding stride at each step based on the model's confidence in the decoded characters, thereby identifying the optimal decoding path for each input. Experimental results demonstrate that FlexReader exhibits robust recognition capability across a diverse range of benchmarks, including those featuring long text, curved text, and occlusions. It consistently outperforms existing decoding strategies in recognition accuracy while achieving up to 6-flod decoding speedup over autoregressive decoding.

</details>


### [139] [Beyond Trial-and-Error: A Geospatial-Guided Active Reasoning Framework for Robust Aerial Vision-and-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41446)
*Jiawei Ge,Wenkang Gong,Jiuxin Cao,Bo Liu,Lei Cui,Haoran Wang,Xuelin Zhu,Biwei Cao,Chang Liu,Chen Feng,Ioannis Patras*

Abstract: Aerial Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) requires UAVs to locate language-specified goals in large-scale urban environments, where objects often appear small and visually ambiguous from high-altitude viewpoints. Existing methods largely follow a reactive paradigm common in indoor VLN, repeatedly updating navigation states from local observations while underutilizing the geospatial structure that remains stable during navigation. As a result, visually similar distractors can cause a reactive goal search to repeatedly reinforce locally plausible goal hypotheses based on passively accumulated evidence, leading to unreliable localization. To address this, we propose GeoReasoner, a robust framework that shifts the paradigm of Aerial VLN from reactive trial-and-error to geospatial-guided active reasoning. Specifically, GeoReasoner first performs pre-search geospatial reasoning to derive region-level priors and constrain the goal hypothesis space. Building on these, it further conducts hypothesis-driven exploration to actively acquire discriminative evidence and refine competing goal hypotheses. Finally, consistency-aware goal verification adversarially assesses complementary evidence sources before stopping, hence reducing premature or erroneous goal confirmations. Experiments on the real-world CityNav benchmark demonstrate that GeoReasoner sets a new SOTA among zero-shot Aerial VLN methods while competing favorably with supervised approaches.

</details>

Abstract: We present BDNet, a hierarchical image-classification backbone built around fast image features and slow statistical references. Its baseline deformation operator (BDO) transforms the deviation of a feature from a stage-specific reference through a low-rank projection and a learned channel scale. Rather than computing pairwise token similarity, the branch operates directly on baseline-relative residuals with complexity linear in the token count. Each of four reference buffers accumulates detached stage statistics during training and remains frozen at inference. A four-stage pyramid with depths $(2,2,6,2)$ and widths $(96,192,384,768)$ yields a 20.70M-parameter classifier at $224\times224$ resolution. We organize an ImageNet-1K study around clean classification, eight controlled ablations, and Gaussian-noise, contrast, and brightness perturbations. The central hypothesis is that a slow reference can improve the robustness of a compact representation while retaining a simple tensor implementation. We distinguish this training-time statistical mechanism from input-dependent attention and make its linear inference behavior explicit.

</details>


### [141] [VariaFlow: Uncertainty-Aware Flow Matching for Unsupervised Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41494)

Abstract: Reconstruction-based anomaly detection no longer fails at reconstruction. Generative models produce high-quality pseudo-healthy reconstructions, and the remaining difficulty is the step of scoring residuals. The residual between a scan and its reconstruction has two indistinguishable sources, genuine pathology and the anatomical variation that separates one healthy brain from another. Scoring against a single reconstruction treats both as anomalous, inflating false positives where healthy anatomy varies most while small or subtle lesions are lost in the surrounding reconstruction error. We identify the missing quantity as the predictive variance, how much a healthy reconstruction can vary at each location. We introduce VariaFlow, which estimates this variance from an ensemble of mask-guided restorations under an unconditional flow matching prior, each started from a different noise initialization, and which normalizes the residual by the resulting standard deviation. Few-step flow matching keeps the ensemble computationally affordable, and restoring at two spatial scales captures lesions of different size. Across three brain MRI datasets spanning stroke and tumor, VariaFlow is trained once on healthy anatomy and generalizes across all three, attaining the highest AUROC (0.94, 0.80, 0.78) on each and the highest AUPRC on both cross-domain datasets, exceeding the strongest baseline by 37% and 50%. Under domain shift, the same baseline drops by up to 52% in AUPRC, whereas VariaFlow stays more stable and achieves the best small-lesion Dice on all three datasets.

</details>

Abstract: High-resolution 3D indoor scene generation is crucial for immersive applications such as virtual reality, spatial computing, and robotics simulation. We introduce VoxLution, a coarse-to-fine transformer-based approach that generates high-fidelity indoor scenes with rich details, overcoming the overly smooth geometry typical of existing generative models. First, we generate a low-resolution spatial layout; then, based on this structural information, we progressively refine the scene geometry locally in chunks. Key to our approach is formulating 3D super-resolution as a sequence modeling problem over multi-resolution sparse voxel grids. To achieve this, we introduce a training-free, resolution-invariant tokenization scheme that compactly encodes the geometric information of eight octants into a single decimal number. We formulate the transformer sequence to include multiple resolutions, allowing the coarse layout to guide the generation of fine local details. To ensure coherence across local chunks, we incorporate adjacent chunk boundary information directly into the transformer sequencing. VoxLution achieves scene generation at a finer voxel resolution than existing baselines, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance on standard benchmarks with 59.6% lower FPD and 51.1% lower FID on 3D-FRONT, and 70.5% lower FPD and 73.8% lower FID on Scannet++.

</details>

Abstract: Score-based multimodal music retrieval requires representations that are both discriminative within each modality and compatible across audio recordings, sheet music images, and symbolic scores. This paper investigates the effectiveness of parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) for adapting pretrained music encoders and examines whether modality-specific improvements translate into better multimodal representations. We compare full fine-tuning, LoRA, and QLoRA across nine pretrained audio, image, and symbolic score encoders on TriScore and the Musical Theme Dataset. Encoders are first evaluated and selected through supervised unimodal classification, using frozen-encoder linear probing as a reference. We then introduce a controlled 2x2 design crossing encoder adaptation with task-specific shared projection, comparing frozen and adapted representations before and after projection. LoRA or QLoRA is selected in 14 of 18 modality-target combinations, frequently matching or outperforming full fine-tuning while optimizing substantially fewer parameters. However, these unimodal gains do not transfer uniformly to multimodal classification. Adaptation and projection are complementary for composer classification on TriScore, largely redundant for TriScore instrument and the Musical Theme Dataset instrument and ensemble tasks, and partially interfering for composer classification on the Musical Theme Dataset. The final adapted-and-projected title representation on TriScore supports piece-level retrieval, reaching average Top-5 and Top-10 accuracies of 55.32 and 64.68, respectively. Cross-modal exclusion and neighborhood analyses further reveal strong modality-specific local structure in the projected spaces. Overall, PEFT provides an effective and computationally efficient alternative to full fine-tuning, but improved unimodal discrimination alone does not guarantee better multimodal organization.

</details>


### [144] [TACO: Trajectory-Aware Curvature Observation for Memorization Localization and Mitigation in Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41520)

Abstract: Text-to-image diffusion models can reproduce memorized training content within localized image regions, making precise localization essential for targeted mitigation. However, curvature observations at a single denoising state can contain transient responses, while spatially uniform mitigation may disrupt prompt-relevant content. We present Trajectory-Aware Curvature Observation (TACO), a unified framework for local memorization detection and mitigation in diffusion models. Our empirical analysis reveals that memorization-related curvature becomes pronounced during late-stage denoising and persists at consistent spatial locations across neighboring states. Motivated by these observations, TACO aggregates prompt-dependent curvature responses along the late denoising trajectory and applies latent-guided spatial refinement to improve spatial coherence. The resulting memorization map serves both as a localization signal and as a spatial control signal for mitigation. Specifically, TACO uses this map to steer existing inference-time interventions toward memorization-related regions through spatial reweighting and localized guidance correction. Extensive experiments demonstrate that TACO consistently outperforms competing localization methods, and improves the Pareto trade-off between memorization suppression, semantic alignment, and perceptual quality. These results establish trajectory-aware spatial analysis as an effective link between memorization localization and targeted mitigation.

</details>


### [145] [Scout-Refine-Correct: Teaching Flow Models to Refine Their Own Samples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41598)
*Sergey Karpukhin,Aibek Alanov*

Abstract: The quality of image generation models trained with diffusion or flow matching is largely fixed by the training budget: at inference, the only available lever is the number of sampling steps, and additional steps improve quality only up to a point. We present a training recipe, Scout-Refine-Correct (SRC), that instead gives a flow-matching generator a genuine inference-time refinement capability. At each sampling step the model takes a scout rollout -- $K$ steps ahead along its own trajectory -- extrapolates a full-sample estimate, and feeds that estimate back to itself as a self-conditioning signal before taking the real step. This can be repeated for $R$ refinement rounds. Two correction losses, applied during training at states the model's own rollouts actually reach rather than only on the ground-truth path, teach the model to use this signal to reach new samples instead of collapsing to a suboptimal one. On ImageNet-256, SRC improves LightningDiT-B from FID~14.5 to FID~7.26 at NFE~550, matching a privileged best-of-$N$ baseline that uses an oracle FID verifier to choose among samples. SRC uses no external verifier, no reward signal, and, after ablation, almost no extra parameters over the base architecture. Combining SRC with best-of-$N$ improves the result further, to FID~3.81 at NFE~4400, showing that the two axes are complementary. We ablate the main design choices -- the architectural pathway for the scout signal, the scout horizon, each correction loss, and the training schedule to demonstrate key working components of training.

</details>

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has recently been extended to vision-language models (VLMs), yet its effectiveness remains limited for text-rich reasoning tasks. We highlight a key factor contributing to this limitation: models frequently arrive at correct final answers despite misperceiving visual information during intermediate steps. We define this phenomenon as Correct-but-Visually-Misgrounded Reasoning (CVMR). Because standard RLVR rewards only final-answer correctness, it fails to differentiate between properly grounded and misgrounded reasoning, reinforcing both equally. Through token-level analysis, we reveal that CVMR tokens exhibit distinct attention patterns that differ systematically from well-grounded ones. Based on this observation, we propose GRIT, a training framework that incorporates visual grounding into RLVR to mitigate the impact of CVMR. Furthermore, to address the lack of text-rich reasoning benchmarks that allow for systematic evaluation across text length and reasoning depth, we introduce GRIT-Bench. Experiments demonstrate that GRIT significantly improves both reasoning accuracy and grounding fidelity over existing RLVR methods.

</details>

Abstract: RGB-D SLAM requires accurate online tracking, high-quality rendering, and geometrically consistent reconstruction. Existing Gaussian SLAM methods mainly optimize Gaussian primitives with photometric, depth, or local frame-to-model constraints, which are often insufficient in indoor scenes with weak textures, large planar structures, noisy depth, and long trajectories. The resulting maps may suffer from pose drift, surface thickness artifacts, noisy normals, and blurred structural boundaries. We propose DrawSplat, an Atlanta World-guided implicit Gaussian SLAM framework for indoor reconstruction. DrawSplat follows a drawing-like process: explicit point-line geometry first sketches the structural layout, and implicit neural Gaussians then fill in high-fidelity appearance and continuous geometric details. It estimates camera poses, sparse geometry, and gravity direction through point-line joint tracking, infers structural planes from tracked lines using the shared-gravity component of the Atlanta World prior, and converts them into differentiable regularization terms on Gaussian centers and normals. To improve compactness and efficiency, DrawSplat further combines an anchor-based implicit neural Gaussian representation with adaptive structural sampling, which allocates Gaussian capacity to structurally informative regions. Experiments on multiple datasets show that DrawSplat achieves robust tracking, high-fidelity rendering, and strong geometric reconstruction among Gaussian-based SLAM systems, while maintaining a practical balance among quality, efficiency, and model size.

</details>


### [148] [Attention Maps Know When to Think: Training-Free Difficulty-Aware Chain-of-Thought for Reasoning Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41618)
*Chanseul Cho,Jaesung Jun,Dongheon Lee,Youngmin Ro*

Abstract: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting has become a standard reasoning strategy in Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs), showing strong performance in complex vision-language tasks such as reasoning segmentation. However, our analysis reveals that indiscriminate CoT usage can lead to degraded performance by inducing unnecessary reasoning, a phenomenon we term overthinking. In reasoning segmentation, overthinking often leads to misalignment between the query's intent and visual grounding. To address this, we propose Difficulty-Aware Chain-of-Thought (DA-CoT), a training-free adaptive framework that selectively triggers CoT based on the input's intrinsic difficulty. We find that the entropy of the model's attention maps reliably reflects task difficulty: low entropy indicates confident, localized grounding, while high entropy suggests uncertainty and potential benefit from CoT. DA-CoT uses this entropy signal to determine whether to apply CoT prompting. Additionally, we introduce a Bidirectional Rethinking mechanism that enables the model to automatically flip its reasoning decision when the initial output fails, enhancing robustness by recovering from failures induced by overthinking and underthinking. DA-CoT demonstrates its effectiveness by achieving state-of-the-art performance on the Reasoning Segmentation benchmarks while significantly improving inference efficiency.

</details>

Abstract: We present TADP, a temporally adaptive differential privacy (DP) framework for inference-time privacy-preserving video generation under fixed-length clip-replacement adjacency. TADP uses a dual-branch design to extract identity and expression representations, clips and privatizes both branches with temporally adaptive Gaussian mechanisms whose noise scales depend only on preceding privatized releases within the same clip and are bounded below by history-uniform calibrated minima, and accounts for their joint release via adaptive Rényi Differential Privacy (RDP) composition. The clip-level RDP guarantee is converted to $(\varepsilon,δ)$-differential privacy, while soft quantization and subsequent temporal processing are applied only after privatization as DP post-processing. Experiments on facial expression datasets show reduced identity leakage under evaluated attacks, usable perceptual quality and temporal stability, and high facial expression recognition utility relative to the evaluated privacy-preserving baselines. TADP formally satisfies clip-level DP and empirically provides a favorable privacy--utility trade-off under the evaluated threat models and datasets.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have made significant progress in spatial reasoning, particularly by integrating RGB images with depth data, which is often assumed to faithfully reflect physical distances. However, mirrors violate this assumption: they distort depth readings while reproducing object appearances, making reflections difficult to distinguish from real objects. As existing benchmarks rarely consider such reflective scenarios, VLM performance in mirror scenes remains largely unexplored. In this work, we introduce a mirror-centric RGB-D dataset from reflective scenes to evaluate the spatial reasoning capabilities of state-of-the-art VLMs in these scenarios. Specifically, we collect 1,011 RGB-D images from 525 real-world scenes, spanning diverse indoor and outdoor environments, six reflective surface types, and both daytime and nighttime conditions. All images are annotated with reflective surface region masks and mask annotations for both directly observed and mirror-reflected objects. Then, we curate a benchmark named MIRA-Bench (Mirror-Induced Reasoning Ambiguity Benchmark), comprising 14,140 QA pairs across eight sub-tasks organized into three cognitive levels: Reflection-aware Perception, Spatial Reasoning, and Scene-level Decision-making. We evaluate state-of-the-art VLMs on MIRA-Bench and find consistent failures across tasks: models misclassify whether objects are directly observed or reflected, fail to match reflected instances to their real-world counterparts, and produce erroneous spatial inferences. Remarkably, providing depth data actually degrades reasoning performance, implying that VLMs cannot identify incorrect depth in mirror scenes. The code and data are available at https://github.com/EvaYin-cloud/MIRA-Bench

</details>

Abstract: Pose estimation is a computer vision task used to estimate a skeleton of dynamic systems to predict future movements. Most of the research in this direction is based on a supervised learning approach which requires a massive amount of labeled datasets. In this paper, a self-supervised three-stage model based on a contrastive learning approach is introduced for estimating a skeleton of dynamic construction machines; such as excavators without using any labeled images for the first stage. The whole model structure is divided into three stages: the pre-train stage using the SimCLR contrastive approach, and two fine-tuning stages for the transfer learning and the downstream task. The model can leverage the features and learn from a huge unlabeled dataset called ACID to two small datasets generated from NVIDIA Isaac and MATLAB Simscape simulators as well as transfer the knowledge to a smaller dataset with a ratio of 3.5% from the original ACID dataset. The results show that the proposed approach can improve the accuracy of pose estimation for heavy construction machines in real images by 11% and 13% in comparison to the normal self-supervised approach with two backbones ResNet-50 and HRNet-W32, respectively.

</details>

Abstract: Unified Multimodal Models (UMMs) achieve remarkable success on diverse multimodal understanding and generation tasks, yet they still struggle to effectively leverage language-based chain-of-thought reasoning for image generation. As a result, existing reasoning-augmented UMMs often fail to inherit the test-time scaling and fine-grained controllability observed in large language models. We attribute this failure to a fundamental modality mismatch: reasoning is performed in a discrete token space, whereas image generation operates in a continuous space. To bridge this gap, we introduce a Latent Reasoning framework in Continuous space (LARC), which alleviates the token-level bottleneck by reasoning over continuous hidden states. Specifically, the training framework consists of two stages: (i) curriculum supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and (ii) information-gain reinforcement learning (RL). First, the curriculum SFT stage gradually converts token-level reasoning into latent reasoning. Subsequently, the information-gain RL stage self-evolves latent reasoning without ground truth supervision. Experiments across diverse text-to-image generation benchmarks show that LARC consistently improves generation quality, prompt following, and compositional fidelity. Notably, LARC achieves state-of-the-art performance on GenEval with a 7%p gain over BAGEL and demonstrates clear test-time scaling behavior, outperforming token-based generation under both parallel and sequential test-time scaling strategies.

</details>


### [153] [OT-CAL: Optimal-Transport-Guided Adaptive Sample Selection for Efficient Visual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41677)

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Deep neural networks for image recognition are typically trained using mini-batches, with a forward pass to compute predictions and losses followed by a more computationally expensive backward pass to update model parameters. However, not all training samples contribute equally to learning, suggesting that computation can be reduced by performing backward passes only on informative samples. The challenge is that sample informativeness changes throughout training: difficult samples may be valuable early on, improving samples become important later, and class diversity becomes increasingly relevant toward the end. Existing methods often rely on fixed selection rules or computationally expensive per-sample gradient calculations. We propose OT-CAL, an adaptive data selection method that automatically adjusts its sampling preferences during training. OT-CAL represents each sample using five informativeness features, including difficulty, learnability, and uncertainty. A curriculum vector describes the currently preferred sample characteristics, and optimal-transport-based matching assigns selection weights according to how closely samples match this target. The curriculum vector is then updated using the selected samples, forming a feedback loop that adapts without a manually defined schedule. Experiments on three standard visual learning benchmarks show that, when selecting 50% of each mini-batch, OT-CAL improves performance by 1.3--2.1 points over AdaSelection while achieving 1.18--1.24 times faster training than full-data training.

</details>

Abstract: Flow models have recently emerged as powerful generative priors for solving inverse problems. Despite their success, most existing flow-based inverse problem solvers initialize the reconstruction process with random Gaussian noise. However, recent studies in image generation have shown that the choice of initial noise can significantly influence generation outcomes. The role of initialization as a distinct design choice in flow-based inverse problem solving remains comparatively underexplored. In this work, we systematically evaluate noise initialization strategies while holding the downstream flow-based inverse solver fixed. We propose Retrieval-Augmented Initialization with Dual-Guided Inversion (RA-DGI), a method that constructs improved initial noise by leveraging retrieved proxy clean images and dual-guided inversion. Extensive experiments across multiple inverse problems demonstrate that the quality of the initial noise substantially affects reconstruction fidelity and perceptual performance. RA-DGI outperforms standard random initialization and achieves superior results compared to inference-time scaling strategies, while requiring less computational overhead. These findings highlight initialization as a critical yet previously overlooked factor in flow-based inverse problem solvers.

</details>

Abstract: Underwater imagery records rich ecological information and supports applications such as science communication, fishery monitoring, ecological conservation, and oceanographic research. Beyond general scene understanding, these applications require models to recognize fine-grained biological traits and reason over spatially grounded evidence. However, existing underwater datasets often provide coarse category labels, generic captions, or total-count supervision, which limits their use for organism-level analysis and ecological reasoning. We introduce Nereus, a large-scale dataset for Underwater Fine-grained Perception that jointly supports Fine-Grained Object Attribute Understanding and Grounded Counting Perception. Nereus contains 88K images and 2.68M question-answer pairs, including annotations for 136K marine organism objects across 2,690 species and 1,515 genera, as well as 65K point annotations and 67K grounded counting QA pairs. Experiments with representative multimodal models show that Nereus improves downstream adaptation over existing underwater-domain data and provides a foundation for future research on fine-grained marine perception.

Abstract: Multitemporal synthetic aperture radar (SAR) imagery provides continuous, cloud-free observations of crop phenology for agricultural monitoring in cloudy and rainy regions, yet high-accuracy classification typically requires extensive labeled samples. Although self-supervised pre-training mitigates label dependency in remote sensing, existing frameworks primarily target optical imagery or generic spatial SAR scenes, underutilizing temporal backscatter dynamics and polarimetric scattering mechanisms. We propose SAR-TS-BERT, a physics-guided BERT-style self-supervised pre-training framework for SAR time-series crop classification. SAR-TS-BERT integrates backscatter intensities and polarimetric ratio features (VV, VH, and VV/VH) with dual-polarimetric Cloude-Pottier decomposition parameters ($H/α/A$) into a six-band input representation through two core designs: 1) a physics-guided span masking strategy (SpanPhysics) that adaptively selects contiguous masked spans based on temporal backscatter change rates to prioritize key phenological transitions; and 2) a dual-branch cross-modal fusion encoder that models backscatter and polarimetric decomposition features through separate Transformer branches followed by bidirectional cross-attention fusion. On independent test sets in Estonia (EuroCropsML) and the USA (California CDL), SAR-TS-BERT achieves overall accuracies (OAs) of 96.78% and 87.29%, respectively, and attains a mean monthly OA of 99.37% on Chongming Island, Shanghai. On the Estonia benchmark, pre-training improves OA and Macro F1 by 1.17 and 1.59 percentage points over training from scratch under full supervision, and by 2.58 and 5.19 percentage points using only 1% labeled data. Incorporating SAR scattering physics into temporal self-supervised pre-training effectively enhances crop classification accuracy, parcel-level mapping consistency, and few-shot transferability.

</details>

Abstract: Reliable histopathology segmentation requires models that rely on pathology-relevant structures rather than spurious background correlations. In this work, we argue that improving architectures alone is insufficient if models continue to rely on spurious correlations rather than pathology-relevant features. We introduce a causality-inspired evaluation framework based on controlled interventions that explicitly probes model sensitivity to foreground tissue structure and background/domain variation. Building on this, we propose the Causal Reliance Score (CRS), a metric that quantifies the relative sensitivity of model predictions to foreground perturbation versus background/domain variation under controlled interventions. To support this analysis, we design augmentation strategies that act as implicit counterfactual interventions during training, encouraging invariance to nuisance factors while preserving morphology. We demonstrate how these interventions reveal behavioural differences between models that are not captured by standard metrics such as Dice or IoU. While we instantiate our framework using a self-supervised transformer (CAUSE-ViT), the primary contribution of this work is not a new architecture, but a principled intervention-based methodology and evaluation metric for auditing model behaviour in medical image segmentation. Our results suggest that evaluating model behaviour through counterfactual interventions is essential for developing reliable and clinically robust medical image segmentation systems.

</details>

Abstract: Highway work zones are critical edge cases for autonomous vehicles (AVs), combining atypical geometries and jurisdiction-specific regulations that existing benchmarks fail to capture: real-world datasets lack 360° multi-modal, full-scene 3D work zone annotation, and no single recorded site can span the layouts and regulatory variants needed for generalizable models. We present WorkIng, a dual-domain benchmark and training resource. WorkIng-Highway provides a 360° multi-modal work zone benchmark with synchronized 128-channel LiDAR, six RGB cameras, and RTK-GPS across 32 km, yielding 12,000+ annotated frames and 227,399 tracked 3D bounding boxes. WorkIng-CARLA complements this with procedural simulation spanning 24 regulation-aware scenarios across six canonical topologies and six international traffic standards, populated by 100+ custom 3D assets. Benchmarking representative 3D detection and end-to-end planning architectures shows that real-world data remains the primary performance driver, and that scenario-stratified synthetic augmentation improves architectures across 2D recognition, 3D detection, and trajectory planning.
