Abstract: Architectural spaces are increasingly gaining robotic capabilities. Architectural robots such as robotic partitions, ceilings, furniture, facades, and other spatial components can autonomously move and reconfigure in response to occupants' needs and activities. Yet, when architectural robots start shaping the spatial conditions in which people continuously live, work, and interact, we may begin to experience the architectural space itself as robotic. What does this shift mean for how we inhabit such spaces? Drawing on our previous studies, I reflect on how architectural robots interact with people not only through their motions, but also through the spatial configurations that persist after those motions stop; how their actions may benefit some occupants while disadvantaging others; and how multiple robots may together make a whole architectural space appear to act. I argue that as computation gains the capacity to physically reconfigure our environments, interaction extends beyond the moment of computational action into the spatial conditions that people continue to inhabit. Understanding robotic architecture therefore requires us to look beyond short-term human-robot interaction and toward how people inhabit, negotiate, and maintain control over spaces that can autonomously reconfigure.

</details>

Abstract: This paper leverages the higher-order kinematic derivatives of serial robots to achieve efficient, aspect-preserving real-time inverse kinematics. First, a family of higher-order solvers is derived, with a necessary orientation-error correction map, yielding the QuIK4 and QuIK5 methods with fourth- and fifth-order convergence. Second, an aspect-preserving step limiter extrapolates the Jacobian determinant along the step to predict and avoid singularities, shortening steps only when they head toward a singularity rather than when the current iterate is ill-conditioned. Third, a Hermite-interpolation certifier verifies the aspect of each answer, and a certified restart algorithm recovers failures from proven same-aspect seeds. The proposed methods are shown to outperform prior methods, reducing failures by up to four orders of magnitude and nearly eliminating aspect switching at tracking-level errors. A C++ library is released with ROS 2 integration.

</details>

Abstract: Recent years have seen multiple impressive results in visual-inertial odometry (VIO) techniques, by which accurate state estimation can be achieved via the extended Kalman filter (EKF) or nonlinear optimization. However, these approaches are rarely open source, and they tend to fail in real experiments due to the temporary lack of feature points and fast motion. Therefore, in this study, we used encoders to overcome the temporary failure of purely vision-based simultaneous localization and mapping (SLAM). Here, we propose a generative measurement model for encoders and derive an expression for the maximum a posteriori (MAP) estimate and necessary Jacobians for optimization. We use our theory to present a novel tightly coupled visual-inertial encoder RGB-Depth (RGB-D) SLAM system. Tests on our system on an in-house dataset confirmed that our modeling effort led to accurate (with a root mean squared error (RMSE) of approximately 2-7 cm) and robust state estimation in real time. The source code and our dataset containing the encoder information have been published for verification.

</details>

Abstract: Recent developments in both high-thrust (HT) and low-thrust (LT) propulsion technologies broaden design options for on-orbit servicing (OOS) missions. However, incorporating LT transfers into OOS mission planning requires accounting for the trade-off between prescribed transfer duration and propellant consumption. Multi-servicing, multi-transfer OOS mission planning is inherently sequential and requires adaptive planning tools that account for propulsion-dependent transfer durations and mass-dependent propellant consumption. Thus, transfer dynamics must be integrated into mission planning and scheduling to increase flexibility and adaptability. In addition, the planning is often done with imperfect information about the system's state and stochastic service demands. This paper focuses on modeling orbital transfers, particularly LT transfers, within a Partially Observable Markov Decision Process (POMDP) framework for OOS mission planning. To enable offline policy computation, the transfer model must provide explicit state transitions and rewards for all admissible actions. The state space is therefore augmented with the servicing vehicle's remaining propellant to enable consistent mass updates. HT transfers are modeled using a precomputed transfer matrix that maps each transfer to the time of flight and propellant consumed. LT transfers are represented using an analytical GEO phasing model with prescribed transfer-duration alternatives. For each alternative, the model calculates propellant consumption and maneuver feasibility as functions of the required phase change and the current servicing-vehicle mass.  The proposed modeling approach is evaluated on simplified OOS planning scenarios using circular phasing with HT and LT propulsion. The resulting POMDPs are solved using the Successive Approximations of the Reachable Space under Optimal Policies (SARSOP) algorithm, and the policies obtained under HT and LT are compared. This comparison provides a quantitative assessment of the feasibility and operational impact of LT options, particularly regarding the potential role of advanced electric propulsion in future servicing architectures.

</details>

Abstract: Inserting an unseen part requires sub-millimetre precision and the correct orientation of keyed features. Current zero-shot agentic manipulation methods built on vision-language models (VLMs) produce spatial outputs that are far too coarse for this. We propose to let a VLM build a sketch model of the task, i.e., a few geometric primitives and two mate frames, which a VLM agent measures with geometric tools in multi-view depth and which a standard planner and compliant controller execute. Because a sketch describes the parts independently of their placement, the robot needs to build it only once per task. A sketch memory stores each agent run and transfers the stored sketch to later placements by point-cloud registration, so that the VLM is called only for unfamiliar parts. After grasping the part, the robot inspects both mating faces at close range and corrects the sketch by shape matching. On 69 simulated insertion episodes, our method inserts 88% of the parts at the assembled pose in a strict kinematic test, while ReKep and MOKA with the same VLM insert none. On new placements of eight tasks, reusing one stored run inserts as many parts as calling the agent in each episode, and it produces the sketch in 3.4 s instead of about 90 s.

</details>


### [268] [When the Simulator Fails, Not the Policy: Auditing Contact Artifacts in Simulated Robot Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41857)
*Alex Zhou,Junhao Wu,Xueyang Zhou,Yixiao Huo,Hongcheng Gao,Di Zhu,Zhenghao Zhang,Yongchao Chen*

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Simulation benchmarks score robot policies by success rate, and every failed episode counts against the policy. Many of these failures are the simulator's fault, not the policy's; we find the cause and fix it. Testing a vision language action model and a world action model in simulation, we find that the policy often places the object and the simulator then ejects it, and that the benchmark's own scripted demonstrations fail the same way. We present SimuGuard, an auditing plugin for LIBERO, RoboTwin, RoboCasa and RoboDojo. It leaves the evaluation unchanged. At every physics step, it records the state and flags an object that starts moving at a contact faster than the robot's contact or gravity account for. It replays a recorded episode bit for bit, and it reruns the episode with one simulation parameter changed. With it we trace these episodes on RoboTwin to the collision detection of the physics engine: for geometry one to two centimeters apart, the engine reports a penetration of about two centimeters, and the solver throws the object out at several meters per second. The cause is one constant: the distance algorithm discards triangles below a fixed area threshold, which valid triangles fall below at the centimeter scale. With that constant changed and nothing else, episodes with an artifact fall from 12 in 100 to none, and the success rate on five container tasks rises from 89% to 98%. Success rates in simulation thus count failures that the simulator causes. Benchmarks on PhysX 5.3.1 or earlier should rerun in a repaired engine. Where the engine cannot change, they should report the success rate together with the range it spans when every flagged episode fails or passes: 75.8% to 98.5% around a reported 83.1% for LingBot-VA.

</details>

Abstract: LLM-based robotic agent systems can generate programs for robot control, yet their generalization to new tasks often depends on elaborate scaffolding. Expanding their skill repertoires alone offers limited guidance on how to assess new capabilities or identify what remains beyond their reach. We introduce META-Robot, a framework that couples self cognition with capability scaling by expanding the criteria through which an agent evaluates and develops its skills. At its core, action-level predicates make execution outcomes explicit and available for LLM reasoning. The agent uses these assessments to identify its own limitations, extend relevant evaluation criteria, and guide the structured expansion of its skills. Proprioceptive feedback further grounds this process in the robot's physical state, enriching the evidence available for self-cognition. Experiments in simulation and on real robots demonstrate stronger overall task performance, generalization to new tasks, continued expansion of skills and evaluation criteria, and effective use of non-visual proprioceptive signals.

</details>

Abstract: Scaling humanoid motion foundation models is constrained by the scarcity of diverse, robot-executable data and by the difficulty of jointly learning from heterogeneous multimodal inputs while supporting real-time closed-loop control. We present Movens-5M, constructed from raw Internet videos through clip generation, quality filtering, semantic annotation, motion recovery, and physical refinement with humanoid retargeting and execution verification. It contains 15 million human-motion sequences and 5 million physically executable humanoid-motion sequences spanning over 10,000 hours, aligned with text, RGB video, speech, and music. Building on this data, we introduce Movens-Zero, a scalable understanding-generation-execution architecture that supports joint learning across modalities and real-time closed-loop execution. Task-mixed flow-matching training jointly optimizes a single action expert across seven generation tasks, enabling direct motion generation from text, speech, music, and video. Multimodal Real-Time Chunking connects overlapping chunks under continuously updated instructions and robot states for continuous closed-loop execution. We further introduce Movens-Intent, a four-track benchmark with 8,192 expert-verified condition-motion pairs across text, speech, music, and video. Experiments show consistent gains across all four modalities when scaling execution-verified data from 1% to 100% and action-expert capacity from 64M to 911M parameters, while demonstrating that data quality and cross-modal supervision improve generation and execution.

</details>

Abstract: How to reuse a pretrained locomotion policy for precise loco-manipulation is still an open problem. We propose an online method to learn the closed-loop dynamics under a frozen locomotion policy. The learned model predicts floating-base motion and improves loco-manipulation accuracy. However, unobserved latent conditions of the policy introduce heterogeneous responses, meaning that the same observed state will lead to different motions. Direct learning without accounting for this heterogeneity introduces bias and fluctuations into dynamics estimates, degrading floating-base prediction and end-effector tracking. To address this problem, a contaminated Gaussian model is proposed to approximate the effects of latent conditions. The model uses two Gaussian components to approximate predictable and unpredictable responses under the same observed state. Then, the contaminated Gaussian model is combined with a Gaussian process to construct an online learner using random Fourier features (RFFs). The learner estimates each response's probability of belonging to the predictable component and uses it to weight online updates, capturing predictable responses while reducing bias and fluctuations caused by heterogeneous observations. With consistent and unbiased dynamics estimates, model predictive control (MPC) predicts floating-base motion accurately and enables precise loco-manipulation. A finite-sample error bound is established to ensure uniform convergence in online learning. Simulation and hardware experiments demonstrate consistent end-effector tracking across different manipulator configurations and tasks, with errors of 0.01 m.

</details>

Abstract: This paper extends an existing framework for the coordination of mobile agents in unknown worlds, by considering the possibility of dynamic instability. In the original problem, known as the Partially Observable Cooperative Guard Art Gallery Problem (POCGAGP) (arXiv:2512.09495), the multi-agent system must establish a communication network which completely covers an initially unknown constrained space. However, the POCGAGP fails to account for the unpredictability often seen in real-world deployment. To address this, the dynamic case (DPOCGAGP) introduces two settings which can influence the mobile network during deployment: Agent Loss and World Change, which represent dynamic changes to the world. Within this study, we present advancements to two state-of-the-art algorithms, the Centralized Agent-Directed Exploration for Network Coverage (CADENCE), and its decentralized analogue, DADENCE, to provide solutions in these cases. We then compare their performance against another state-of-the-art benchmark algorithm, and show that the adapted algorithms outperform across our benchmark test set, which comprises over 4500 test cases of varied size and structure.

</details>

Abstract: Automated endoscope guidance reduces the need for continuous manual camera control during minimally invasive surgery. However, the desired camera motion depends on the surgeon's immediate intentions and cannot always be inferred from the visual operative scene alone. We propose to automate the task using language-conditioned behavioral cloning; we define 6 clinically relevant guidance behaviors and collect 235 subtask demonstrations. In preliminary phantom experiments, our policy achieves 100% success on focus, overview, and stop, 80% on search-tip and follow-tip behaviors, and 50% on horizon leveling. These results demonstrate the feasibility of learning heterogeneous surgeon-requested camera behaviors with a single visuomotor policy, while the observed failure modes motivate future work on adding temporal context for more demanding guidance tasks.

</details>

Abstract: Data-driven controllers have enabled lower-limb robotic exoskeletons to adapt assistance to the user across many tasks. A leading example is biological torque control, which estimates the user's own joint moment from onboard sensors and assists in proportion to that estimate. However, its estimators are trained on data recorded with the target device, so deploying the controller on new hardware requires collecting new data. Existing open-source efforts release hardware, software, or controllers separately, so this data collection must still be repeated on the assembled device. Here, we present an open-source knee exoskeleton, built from off-the-shelf components and widely available fabrication tools, with a biological torque controller derived entirely from simulation. A muscle-driven imitation policy reproduces squatting motions from an open biomechanics dataset, and the resulting knee moments are distilled into an estimator that uses only onboard sensors, so no training data are recorded on the device. In seven able-bodied participants, assistance reduced peak biological knee extension moment by 21.5% and peak vastus medialis activation by 26.7% during squatting, relative to squatting without the exoskeleton (p<0.05). Our results demonstrate that a biological torque controller derived from simulation can transfer to physical hardware without device-specific data collection. Because the hardware and training pipeline are released together, research groups can train and compare knee exoskeleton controllers on shared hardware without additional data collection overhead.

</details>

Abstract: Robotic Manipulation Surfaces (RMS) reposition objects by deforming the surface on which they rest. A recent class of RMS suspends a continuous fabric sheet from a sparse grid of vertical linear actuators lowering the actuator density of such surfaces but how to design such surfaces so that objects can be controlled everywhere on it, and how to extend it beyond a single module so that objects can pass between modules and achieve parallel, remain open. This paper presents a separable catenary model of the suspended sheet that is fast to compute and provides both the surface shape for given actuator heights and the actuator heights for a desired local slope. The closed form inverse lets a simple PID controller position objects on the sheet, and the model relates fabric length, module span and actuator stroke to the reachable workspace, yielding a minimum fabric length bound and design charts for these surfaces. The approach is then extended to a multimodular platform in which neighbouring modules share actuators. On a $2 \times 2$ prototype, we demonstrate that centimetre-scale objects are transferred between modules and positioned within 0.03 m over a $1 m^2$ workspace with only nine actuators, enabling multi-object manipulation and opening the way to parallel manipulation.

</details>

Abstract: Large-scale egocentric human videos provide rich and scalable manipulation experience for robot policy learning, yet substantial embodiment gaps in visual appearance, kinematic structure, and action spaces hinder effective knowledge transfer. Existing methods often decouple cross-embodiment knowledge alignment from robot policy learning, making it difficult for human manipulation knowledge to directly and fully contribute to robot action learning. To address this issue, we propose Nexus-VLA, a hybrid policy learning framework that enables cross-embodiment knowledge transfer through end-to-end human-robot joint training, allowing human manipulation experience to directly contribute to robot policy learning. We first introduce a hierarchical cross-embodiment semantic alignment paradigm that progressively establishes shared manipulation semantics from task intent to fine-grained interactions, enabling more complete use of the manipulation knowledge contained in human videos. Building on this hierarchy, we design a shared manipulation latent representation that jointly encodes multi-level shared knowledge into an internal representation directly used for continuous action generation, improving policy learning efficiency while effectively leveraging human knowledge. Across RoboCasa-GR1, OOD settings, and real-world robot experiments, Nexus-VLA achieves an 80.5% task success rate on RoboCasa-GR1 and demonstrates strong advantages in human-data utilization, learning efficiency, and cross-scenario generalization.

</details>

Abstract: Foundation models are becoming remarkably reliable at high-level reasoning, yet they remain too slow and costly to serve as fine-grained controllers. Conversely, fast policies like VLAs and whole-body controllers offer more efficient reflexes, but struggle to generalize. While pairing a deliberative brain (foundation models) with a reactive cerebellum (fast policies) seems natural for embodied systems, two obstacles remain: methods are built and scored on incompatible stacks, and their asynchronous coordination is difficult. For example, an agent that calls the VLA in the agentic loop can stall the fast execution, while a VLA that calls the agent on errors tends to re-trigger fallbacks repeatedly or completely miss silent failures. To address this, we propose REA (Routed Embodied Agent), a unified runtime where a deliberative System 2, an embodied router System 1 and reactive System 0 policies cooperate under a single golden rule - no running policy waits on the brain. Instead of querying the brain at each failure, REA employs a route memory that stores each policy's measured success ahead of time, a boundary-triggered judge gate that consults the brain on policy execution, and a shared-budget escalation ladder for bounded recovery. Evaluated on 3,000 test episodes across 60 LIBERO-Pro tasks, REA reaches 95.9% success and beats the best single-agent baseline in its own policy pool (94.7%) while reducing control steps by 25% and control time by 17%. In a separate 100-episode RoboTwin 2.0 study, REA achieves 84% success with VLA policies and 86% with VLA+code, compared with 80% for the strongest single VLA baseline MEDex. These results demonstrate learned and programmatic policies operating within the same runtime. REA also transfers to BEHAVIOR-1K and a real Unitree G1 humanoid, achieving the highest observed success among the compared methods.

</details>

Abstract: A fallen humanoid beneath an overhead obstacle faces a coupled recovery problem. It must create clearance while organizing support for a stable rise, since attempting to stand before making room can further increase obstruction. We introduce a reinforcement learning method with contact-supported geometric escape guidance, which rewards incremental reduction of robot-obstacle overlap without prescribing an escape path. To achieve this, a learned motion prior, together with posture-dependent support and joint-stall costs, is proposed to shape a single policy that learns when to clear the obstacle, how to establish support, and when to complete the rise, all using proprioception alone. In simulation, it recovers in 88.5% of trials beneath plates that can only be lifted, compared with 18.9% without geometric guidance, and in 98.6% of trials beneath free plates with randomized size, mass and placement. On a Unitree G1, the deployed policy recovered in 95 of 150 attempts across seven real-world obstacle scenes, including furniture and multi-object arrangements absent from training. Project page: https://lu-yidan.github.io/ROOF/

</details>

Abstract: Planning with world models enables test-time adaptation for embodied agents, offering superior generalization over direct policy learning. However, long-horizon tasks remain hindered by cumulative errors and search complexity. Existing hierarchical methods often use rigid macro-actions or latent skills, leading to brittle representations and limited fine-grained control. We introduce ID-WM (Implicit Dynamics World Models), a hierarchical world model that decouples state-space reachability from local control. Our framework utilizes a high-level model to sample feasible transitions across the state manifold and a low-level world model as an inverse dynamics solver to ground these transitions into precise actions. This allows the planner to identify reachable subgoals without being constrained by low-level execution details. By prioritizing local physical feasibility over global behavioral patterns, ID-WM synthesizes novel long-horizon plans from short segments, transcending the specific behavioral modes seen during training. We demonstrate its effectiveness across three navigation and manipulation suites, achieving a 38.5% improvement over state-of-the-art policy and diffusion-based planners. Crucially, by leveraging this hierarchical structure to mitigate compounding errors, our approach yields a 135% performance gain over single-level world model MPC, particularly in complex, unseen configurations for long-horizon tasks. Videos can be viewed at https://id-wm.github.io

</details>


### [280] [RoboArchitect: Automating the Design of Robotic Agents with Persistent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42141)

Abstract: Recent work shows that a frozen robot policy performs far better inside an agent harness, where control code decides when to query the policy, consult vision tools, or command the arm directly. Yet today these harnesses are built by hand and redesigned for each new robot or benchmark. We show that harness design can be automated, offering a strong alternative to finetuning on a novel setup. In RoboArchitect, a coding model observes a robot's rollouts, diagnoses failures, and iteratively revises the harness around the frozen policy. The harness evolves on a set of episodes for its optimization and is evaluated on held-out scenes and tasks. Without updating any model weights, RoboArchitect raises average success from 9.7% to 43.3% on unseen scenes (4.5x) and from 12.7% to 40.2% on unseen tasks (3.2x), across multiple benchmarks and policies. The gains are largest where the benchmark is far from the policy's training data: wrapping a general-purpose policy, the evolved harness yields a specialist that often outperforms even the finetuned version. Intuitive control strategies are automatically designed, such as lifting a jammed arm clear or reaching in horizontally when an obstacle blocks the way from above. The harness also evolves its own persistent memory, carrying learned experience, such as effective grasp depths, into later decisions. These results suggest that substantial headroom in robotic manipulation lies in the agent harness, and its design can be effectively automated and optimized without weight updates.

</details>

Abstract: Future hydrogen systems depend on large-scale storage to balance supply and demand. Hydrogen networks can store energy in the pipelines themselves, but the potential of this linepack storage has not been quantified comparably to dedicated storage facilities. We transfer metrics established for conventional storage, such as volume capacity and full-cycle equivalents, to hydrogen networks. To evaluate them, we formulate an optimization problem maximizing linepack storage utilization, subject to non-convex PDE-based constraints describing transient gas flow. We solve the optimization problem over a full-year horizon to capture seasonal variations and to assess the interaction with cavern storage. For computational tractability, we propose a simulation-informed sequential convex programming approach with adaptive time stepping that reduces problem size by up to 98% at accuracy comparable to fixed-resolution methods. For the GasLib-40 topology with German scenario data, the network provides a volume capacity of 24 million normal cubic meters at 28 full-cycle equivalents per year.

</details>

Abstract: We study a long-run sender who repeatedly communicates about a state to a sequence of myopic receivers and whose credibility is a public score updated from past interactions. Receivers face a lemon loss and an opportunity loss, and their behavior depends on these only through a single index, the tolerance. Because the score records only detected misrepresentations, it rests above the sender's true honesty rate; we characterize this credibility premium in closed form. In a fluid limit in which the score adjusts slowly, no policy earns more than operating at a single credibility level, and the sender's gain from managing credibility equals the value of the premium: against receivers who correct for imperfect detection, that gain vanishes. At a fixed adjustment speed, every policy is bounded by the fluid value plus a term linear in the speed. We then study three policy classes, linear, (s,S), and threshold, obtain the optimal parameters of the linear and threshold classes in closed form, show how receiver losses shape them, and show that, under an approximation, the optimal (s,S) band collapses to a threshold. Simulations of the dynamics agree with the closed forms and indicate where the single-level conclusion stops: bands pay when switching modes is costly, and cycles pay when demand is seasonal.

</details>

Abstract: Communication links influence how strongly a malicious input at one sensor affects a consensus network. We study how a defender can reduce this influence by selecting one additional link after observing the currently attacked sensor. The attacked sensor changes over time, and vulnerability is measured by the trace of a finite-horizon controllability Gramian. A prescribed local epsilon-greedy attacker response rule defines a discounted Markov decision process for the defender. Exact dynamic programming (DP) provides an optimal policy using the full Gramian payoff matrix and attacker transition model. We then introduce approximate dynamic programming (ADP) that learns from sampled attacker transitions and empirical Gramian costs estimated from measured impulse responses. Under conditionally unbiased costs and standard sampling and learning-rate conditions, the learned action-value table converges to the exact DP solution. The analysis uses Bellman contraction and bounds the effect of persistent Gramian estimation bias, establishing when measured responses recover optimal defender decisions without constructing the full payoff matrix. In simulations on 20- and 100-node graphs, empirical ADP rollout costs are within 0.23% and 1.76%, respectively, of exact DP. In a two-dimensional intruder tracking example, both policies reduce network-wide root-mean-square tracking error spread by approximately 25% relative to an unmitigated attack.

</details>

Abstract: Heat-pump (HP)-connected district heating systems (DHSs) can support grid frequency by rapidly modulating HP electrical consumption while accommodating the resulting heat imbalance. Thermal-state-informed HP control improves EPS--DHS coordination, but introduces slower DHS dynamics and heat-side disturbance propagation at the electrical interface on the time scale of secondary frequency control. This paper formulates the regulated DHS as a grid-facing dynamic port and develops a mixed $H_\infty$--passivity framework for disturbance-independent temperature regulation while preserving a clean electrical response during secondary frequency control. Sufficient conditions guarantee local asymptotic stability of the EPS--HP--DHS interconnection across primary frequency control, secondary frequency control, and DHS regulation. These stability conditions are integrated with multi-channel $H_\infty$ attenuation of DHS-state-dependent electrical dynamics, heat-disturbance propagation, and DHS-regulation errors, yielding a tractable convex LMI program for controller synthesis. Cross-model validation deploys controllers synthesized on control-oriented EPS--DHS models, without retuning, on an open-source electromechanical IEEE 39-bus model coupled with four high-fidelity finite-volume DHS models retaining thermal transport, storage, and heat-loss dynamics. The resulting controller improves EPS--DHS coordination without compromising the grid-facing electrical response.

</details>

Abstract: Nonlinear dynamical systems with multiple omega-limit sets pose a fundamental challenge for global finite-dimensional Koopman modeling with continuous observables. A natural way to address this challenge is to decompose the state space into forward-invariant regions associated with distinct asymptotic behaviors and to learn a separate Koopman model for each region. This paper studies how to compute such partitions from data. We establish conditions under which Koopman-invariant observables, namely eigenfunctions associated with the unit eigenvalue, characterize the stationary partition induced by omega-limit sets. Building on this characterization, we develop OPAL (Omega-limit Partitions from Approximate invariants Learned from data), an algorithm that extracts partitions from approximate invariant observables. Numerical examples show that the recovered partitions capture the relevant asymptotic regions and support multiple regional linear Koopman models with significantly improved long-horizon prediction capabilities.

</details>

Abstract: Incomplete long-term contracting in electricity markets can expose investors and consumers to inefficient levels of risk. This paper investigates how incompleteness impacts the optimal choice of climate policy. We develop a game-theoretic model of optimal policy-making in anticipation of how electricity market agents react to a given policy when gas prices and demand are uncertain and contracting is incomplete. In illustrative experiments, we observe that renewable subsidies and carbon prices induce an insurance co-benefit when markets are incomplete. This is because, relative to a complete market benchmark, incompleteness shifts investment from renewables toward gas and exposes consumers to uninsured risk from inter-annual volatility in electricity prices. By incentivizing investment in renewables, subsidies and carbon prices indirectly counteract these distortions. Consequently, incompleteness increases the optimal subsidy and carbon price levels in our experiments. Renewable subsidies exhibit a stronger insurance effect than carbon pricing, as the latter leads to more investment in gas with carbon capture and storage and more volatile electricity prices. While carbon pricing is more economically efficient than subsidies in complete markets, incompleteness shrinks its efficiency advantage and even leads to cases where subsidies are more economically efficient.

</details>

Strategic temporal and spatial shifting capture 75% and 93% of the planner's savings, forgoing the rest mostly by withholding flexibility to protect favorable prices. Scarce capacity and abundant renewables raise this share for temporal shifting; scarce transmission lowers it for spatial shifting. Temporal and spatial flexibility substitute for thermal and transmission capacity, respectively, and complement renewables.

Abstract: Low-voltage ride-through (LVRT) forces a grid-following inverter to allocate finite converter current between active- and reactive-current objectives. This paper uses a PSCAD electromagnetic-transient (EMT) representation of an aggregated battery energy storage system (BESS) to quantify how short-circuit ratio (SCR) and grid impedance X/R ratio alter that tradeoff. Active-current priority and reactive-current priority are compared for five paired grid conditions under the same 150-ms balanced three-phase fault, with pre-fault active power approximately 0.84 pu, POI reactive-power exchange near 0 pu, and a 1.0-pu dq-current limit. Fundamental positive-sequence voltage and power are reconstructed from 100-$μ$s three-phase EMT outputs. Across the fixed-fault SCR sweep, the Q-priority late-fault voltage advantage increases from 0.046 pu in the strongest tested grid to 0.129 pu in the weakest; the weakest P-priority case also shows active-power reversal and a pronounced controller-frequency excursion. At fixed SCR, changing X/R produces a smaller increase from 0.092 to 0.112 pu, mainly through reduced P-priority voltage support. Because the common physical fault also deepens the passive retained-voltage condition as SCR decreases, the SCR result is interpreted as a fixed-fault grid-strength sensitivity rather than a constant-sag comparison. Grid strength is the larger sensitivity in the tested matrix, while X/R remains an additional modifier under the studied fault conditions.

</details>

Abstract: EMT--RMS benchmarking is widely used to verify cross-platform consistency of inverter-based resource (IBR) dynamic models. This paper examines a 200 MW anonymized IBR represented in PSCAD and PSS/E. The EMT model separates inverter electrical capability from a plant-level power plant controller (PPC) recovery target, whereas the RMS model represents these functions through REECC and REPCA parameters. Directly assigning the PPC target of $\pm 0.2764$ pu to REPCA hard reactive limits is initialization-inconsistent at the original lagging operating point because the initial reactive command is 0.396273 pu. A second, near-unity-power-factor operating point initializes successfully with the same diagnostic limit, demonstrating that the conflict is operating-point dependent. Using the non-conflicting capability mapping removes the original hard-limit conflict, but the benchmark still shows 18.5--30.2 MVAr post-event POI reactive-power differences from PSCAD. A one-at-a-time REPCA $K_c$ sensitivity under matched benchmark initial conditions further shows that RMS recovery is parameter- and limit-dependent: at $K_c=0.05$ and 0.10 the aggregate machine reactive output reaches the configured capability bounds, while $K_c=0.15$ moves VDOWN, VUP, and HVRT off those bounds and changes their settled POI reactive power by 8.94, 18.00, and 3.17 MVAr, respectively. Machine-to-POI accounting further shows why machine-side saturation need not reproduce the POI-level PPC recovery magnitude. The results distinguish initialization feasibility from recovery equivalence and provide a control-layer diagnostic workflow beyond waveform comparison.

</details>

Abstract: Generative models are increasingly used for circuit synthesis and optimization by proposing topologies, sizing devices, and exploring large design spaces. We investigate whether they can instead support design discovery, where generated structures must depart from known designs while producing reproducible electrical behavior. We propose Delta-Seeker, a closed-loop circuit search framework that combines discrete diffusion for topology generation and mutation with structural checking, SPICE-in-the-loop sizing, Pareto selection, and feedback from prior search outcomes. The search controller uses progress and recurring structural failures to allocate evaluation effort and steer subsequent topology generation, while independent re-sizing and operating-corner analysis are performed only after search. In generator-only experiments, cross-generation feedback increases structurally valid children from 143 to 945 and reduces the faulty-candidate fraction from 0.925 to 0.761. In op-amp topology discovery at matched sizing cost, Delta-Seeker yields 54 designs that remain feasible in at least three of four independent re-sizings and 18 that additionally pass device-level checks and supply-temperature corner analysis, compared with 38 and 10 under fixed control. Removing cross-generation memory reduces independently robust discoveries from 54 to 43 and seven-corner robust discoveries from 40 to 27. In DC-DC converter search, nearest-parent analysis identifies a corpus-absent topology whose generated switching current path changes its nearest training-corpus parent from infeasible at all four operating corners to feasible at all four, while increasing median efficiency from 31.2% to 98.96%. These results show that closed-loop generative search can support circuit discovery beyond structural novelty by identifying new topologies with independently verified changes in circuit operation.

</details>

Abstract: Battery energy storage system (BESS) flexibility depends on state of charge and intertemporal operating constraints. Renewable forecast errors of similar magnitudes can lead to different BESS dispatch results. An error may alter early charging or discharging decisions and reduce the flexibility available for subsequent regulation, while another may have little effect on the resulting schedule. To account for these unequal consequences, this paper proposes Frequency-Calibrated Decision-Focused Learning (FC-DFL), which trains renewable predictors using the realized operating costs of their induced BESS schedules. Gaussian smoothing and solver-based zeroth-order estimation provide training feedback without requiring differentiating through the non-differentiable mixed-integer BESS dispatch problem. The same decision feedback is further combined with prediction sensitivity to develop a decision-oriented XAI method that identifies the input features and forecast periods most consequential to BESS operation. Case studies using German wind and solar data show that FC-DFL reduces the integrated absolute frequency-deviation metric by 6.05% and decision regret by 23.92% relative to accuracy-based predict-then-optimize method. The XAI analysis further demonstrates that the identified high-impact forecast periods produce 2.84 times larger average absolute changes in realized operating cost than low-impact periods. These findings highlight the importance of incorporating downstream decision-making outcomes into renewable forecasting and demonstrate the value of FC-DFL in improving BESS regulation and providing operationally meaningful forecast explanations.

</details>

Abstract: Artificial intelligence data center (AIDC) campuses contain sustained power variations and subsecond power fluctuations, requiring both sufficient stored energy and fast response. A shared hybrid energy storage system (HESS) can mitigate aggregate AIDC power deviations, but interval-based schedules cannot capture the delay between commanded and delivered power at the campus point of common coupling (PCC). To this end, this paper proposes a multi-timescale scheduling model for mitigating AIDC power fluctuations with the shared HESS. First, it models electrical pooling at the campus bus and constrains the shared HESS to a single PCC injection. Second, a two-layer model passes five-minute scheduled power, energy state, and reserve capacity to a causal fast response model that distinguishes commanded power from delivered power. Case studies using the National Laboratory of the Rockies (NLR) GenAI Power Profiles Dataset show reductions in both PCC and fast integral absolute error (IAE), while retaining most of the improvement available under ideal response.

Abstract: We study Riemannian optimization on an unknown manifold accessed only through feasible samples. The central challenge is to turn feasible samples into optimization steps, i.e., the samples are drawn from the unknown feasible manifold, but do not directly provide the tangent spaces, projections, or retractions required by classic Riemannian methods. Moreover, regions with low objective values may be sparsely represented in the data, so directly sampling from a distribution fitted to these observations can require many draws to find better feasible solutions. We introduce Proximal Refinement on Implicit Smooth Manifolds (PRISM), which turns feasible-sample access into an iterative optimization procedure by repeatedly sampling an objective-aware proximal distribution conditioned on the current iterate. Theoretically, we show that PRISM recovers the effect of Riemannian-gradient refinement, leading to provable objective descent, feasibility (manifold adherence), and convergence toward first-order stationarity. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach through synthetic experiments illustrating its geometric properties and applications to data-driven building control and diverse real world optimization tasks, highlighting its potential as a general framework for data-driven optimization across scientific and engineering domains.

</details>

Abstract: Neuromorphic controllers interact with physical systems through fixed-amplitude spikes whose timing conveys control information. Existing Lyapunov-based greedy spiking controllers guarantee stability but are restricted to linear systems and neglect firing-rate limits. We extend greedy spiking control to nonlinear systems using Koopman operator theory. For observable dictionaries invariant under differentiation along the input direction, spike-induced state translations admit an exact linear representation in lifted coordinates, enabling exact modeling of spiking jump dynamics. This allows firing decisions to be formulated as comparisons of quadratic Lyapunov functions in the lifted space. We prove practical stability of a clocked, rate-limited controller with an explicit ultimate bound and develop a data-driven design based on Koopman eigenfunctions learned from unforced data by generator-based regression. The weights of the Lyapunov function are synthesized by a linear program that enforces a sampled relaxation of the rate-limited control-Lyapunov condition. Applied to an inverted pendulum, the learned eigenfunctions recover the system energy, and the proposed controller achieves swing-up and stabilization from nearly all initial conditions despite severe actuation limits.

</details>

Abstract: Islanded power systems are inherently challenging to operate due to limited interconnection, low inertia, and weak short-circuit strength. As renewable and inverter-based resources replace synchronous machines, these challenges worsen, causing faster frequency deviations, greater voltage volatility, and higher risk of instability. This report summarizes the differences between islanded and interconnected systems and reviews key stability issues in islanded grids: frequency stability, converter-driven interactions, voltage stability, large-disturbance behavior, and small-signal dynamics.
  Grid-forming (GFM) voltage source converters are a promising solution because they can establish voltage and frequency, emulate synchronous machine behavior, and provide grid strength, virtual inertia, fast frequency response, voltage regulation, and black-start capability. The report examines relevant GFM control strategies and evaluates their capabilities in weak, low-inertia islanded grids.
  Challenges remain, particularly interoperability, control coordination among multiple inverter-based resources, lack of standardized grid code requirements, and coordination of protection devices. Successful deployment requires advances in interoperability, harmonized grid codes, and grid planning that considers the unique features of islanded systems, along with clear GFM functionality requirements.
  Finally, a case study illustrates the stability-enhancing capability of GFM converters in weak grids through small-signal stability analysis. Overall, GFM technology is a key enabler of reliable, renewable-dominant island power systems, but broader adoption depends on continued technical and regulatory development.

</details>

Abstract: Pinching antenna (PA) systems offer flexible antenna configurations for future wireless communications, yet how PA geometry, orientation, and position affect channel characteristics in practical environments remains insufficiently studied. To address this gap, this paper first presents an indoor PA system channel measurement campaign at 59.5~GHz. A dedicated time-domain channel sounder is developed with frequency conversion and a circular-waveguide-to-dielectric-waveguide transition to feed the radio frequency (RF) signal into a flexible dielectric waveguide. Using this platform, office measurements quantify channel attenuation and channel characteristics by examining four PA geometries, arc PA orientation, circle PA pinching position, and transmitter--receiver height difference. The channel measurement data are analyzed in terms of path loss, shadow fading, root-mean-square (RMS) delay spread, Rician K-factor, normalized capacity, and spatial similarity. Indoor path loss models for PA systems are fitted for each PA geometry. The results reveal geometry-dependent attenuation and shadow-fading variability, with the arc PA showing greater variability than the circle PA. Arc PA rotation shifts the delay spread and conditional K-factor distributions. Moving the circle PA produces nonmonotonic changes in delay spread and normalized capacity, while spatial similarity remains relatively high at many receiver locations as the pinching position changes. These observations provide an empirical basis for PA systems channel model and system design.

</details>

Abstract: RVPPs that aggregate dispatchable and non-dispatchable renewable energy sources, storage, and flexible demand that bid in the day-ahead and secondary reserve markets must cope with uncertainties in market prices, renewable generation, and demand. MBRO is well suited to this setting because it hedges adverse realizations through several deviation layers while keeping the final bidding problem as a tractable, scenario-free MILP. Its performance, however, depends on how the deviation bounds and uncertainty budgets are calibrated. Existing MBRO implementations commonly rely on fixed percentile-based bounds and externally specified budget vectors, usually examined through sensitivity analysis. This paper proposes PL-MBRO, that constructs parameter-specific deviation bounds from historical data conditional on selected risk-attitude quantiles and automatically derives the corresponding uncertainty budgets from empirical cluster probabilities. For each uncertain parameter, k-medians clustering partitions the historical daily profiles into representative probability layers; the cluster median defines the balanced representative, while favorable and adverse within-cluster percentiles define the optimistic and pessimistic representatives. The empirical cluster probabilities are mapped to integer full-horizon uncertainty budgets over the 96 quarter-hourly periods, while the robust inner problem determines the periods in which the corresponding deviation layers are most adverse to the schedule. Compared with a percentilebased MBRO benchmark, in-sample and out-of-sample tests on an RVPP bidding in both markets show that PL-MBRO consistently achieves better realized economic outcomes across the optimistic, balanced, and pessimistic strategies, with improvements of up to 92.7% in-sample and 57.3% out-of-sample, measured relative to the absolute value of the corresponding benchmark realized profit.

</details>

Abstract: We present Scaly, an open-source optimizing compiler for optimal control, written in Python, that generates fast C code. Scaly provides a modeling language embedded in Python for the parametric optimization problems that arise in model predictive control and moving horizon estimation, and computes the sparse derivatives that standard optimization solvers need. Scaly accepts general nonlinear programs but applies optimizations specific to optimal control, which preserve the repeated structure of a problem, such as the stages of a horizon, through differentiation down to the generated loops. The generated C depends only on the C math library and never allocates memory dynamically. On three benchmark problems, Scaly evaluates the Hessian of the Lagrangian up to 26 times faster than CasADi, with smaller binaries and build times that stay nearly constant with the horizon. With the same solver, Scaly shortens the solver time per control step on every benchmark, by a factor of up to 9.

</details>

Abstract: This paper proposes a maximum a posteriori (MAP) inference framework with a truncated diffusion prior for safe planning (TDP-SafeMAP). Under a generalized Bayesian formulation, a criterion based on covariance condition number determines the reverse diffusion truncation timestep, and the clean control distribution predicted at that timestep is approximated by a Gaussian prior encoding the control regularities and uncertainty of expert data. An energy function containing a control barrier function (CBF) constraint violation measure defines a Gibbs task likelihood that incorporates physical constraint information. After fusing the prior and likelihood, the covariance matrix adaptation evolution strategy (CMA-ES), whose core update admits an interpretation as a sampling-based approximation to natural-gradient ascent, approximates the posterior mode using only black-box evaluations. Bézier-curve-based dimensionality reduction and related techniques further improve computational efficiency and task-completion capability. Theoretical analysis establishes an error bound for the approximate MAP solution, an explicit condition for CBF safety preservation, and the equivalence of the MAP problem under prior whitening. Experimental results show that the proposed framework consistently generates safe, smooth, and goal-reaching trajectories, providing an effective and practical statistical inference approach to safe planning for black-box systems.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-objective optimal control is commonly addressed by reinforcement learning with fixed scalarization weights, requiring a separate training run and constraint enforcement for each trade-off. This letter extends critic-free group-relative policy optimization to terminally constrained multi-objective control: a single preference-conditioned policy is trained over random preferences and returns a feasible approximation of the Pareto front in one run. Three structural results are established. Group normalization after scalarization preserves the prescribed preference, whereas per-objective normalization induces policy-dependent distortion; with pairwise distinct normalization scales, only single-objective preferences remain unchanged. A positive floor prevents the terminal-constraint multiplier from vanishing and renders the penalty exact when the floor exceeds a geometric multiplier. Deterministic terminal violation is bounded by expected stochastic violation plus a term depending on terminal-state curvature and action variance, so stochastic feasibility alone does not certify deterministic execution. On a heavy-duty range-extender powertrain with six stage costs and a terminal state-of-charge band, a representative training run places all 32 preference-grid solutions in band on six cycles inside and outside the training pool. Averaged over ten runs, the method yields more in-band solutions under deterministic execution than a critic-based conditioned baseline with the same terminal-constraint handling.

</details>

Abstract: This paper develops a safety verification framework for inverter-based radial distribution networks under sensor and actuator attacks. Each customer is equipped with a droop-based controller to regulate its voltage within a desirable bound of a reference, i.e., to achieve safety. In our setting, attacks and disturbances enter the droop-based controllers, while actuator attacks corrupt the reactive power injected into the network. Consequently, invariance of the controller state alone does not guarantee compliance with inverter capacities or customer voltage limits. To address this challenge, we derive conditions to guarantee that the reactive power state remains inside a predefined ellipsoid, while at the same time ensuring that the attacked reactive power injections and customer voltages respect predetermined operating limits that hold uniformly for the entire predefined ellipsoid. Together, these conditions guarantee safety under all admissible disturbances and state-dependent sensor and actuator attacks. Finally, we express the resulting containment condition as a linear matrix inequality (LMI) feasibility problem. A five-customer radial network based on the CIGRE benchmark corroborates our theoretical developments in simulations.

</details>

Abstract: The COVID-19 pandemic has exhibited multiple waves of infections, reflecting feedback between disease transmission and adaptive population behavior. Accurately predicting and understanding these dynamics remains a central challenge for epidemic modeling. In this paper, we develop a forecasting framework based on a Susceptible-Exposed-Infectious-Recovered (SEIR) model coupled with an evolutionary model of behavioral adaptation, in which population compliance with protective measures evolves in response to perceived infection risk and associated costs. We formulate forecasting as the estimation of behavioral incentive parameters that govern the coupled behavior-disease dynamics, using hospitalization time series data together with standard assumptions on disease-related parameters. Using the inferred parameters, we generate rolling monthly forecasts of infection levels and evaluate the approach on both synthetic data and hospitalization time series from multiple U.S. states (Texas, California, Colorado, and Arizona). The proposed framework provides a principled way to incorporate behavior-disease feedback into epidemic forecasting while yielding interpretable estimates of population-level incentives.

</details>

Abstract: Dynamic operating envelopes (DOEs) can allocate time-varying export capacity to distributed energy resources (DERs), and while they are not intended to make operations immune to voltage violations, they should guide operations to mostly remain inside expected values under changing regimes and residual forecast errors. However, although recent literature proposed many different techniques for implementing DOEs, there is sparse discussion of analyzing and quantifying the risk of using more or less conservative DOEs under uncertain operating regimes. Having this gap in mind, this paper develops a risk-oriented extension of a previously established fair distributed multi-period optimal power flow (OPF) framework, where fair or technical DOEs are fixed as first-stage decisions and the OPF leverages battery energy storage and renewable curtailment as recourse under regime uncertainties. An independent nonlinear AC power flow then computes a resulting ensemble, which is summarized by (i) voltage-violation probability, severity, and a persistence metric for consecutive out-of-band hours, (ii) curtailment and Jain and Gini indices, and (iii) model deviations. Different probability situations are assessed, ranging from uniform, sunny-heavy, night-peak-heavy, and adversarial, which separate the envelope design from the assumed operating distribution. An optional outer loop allows for fine-tuning a fairness--efficiency parameter under explicit violation and persistence budgets. The combination of the outer loop and the overall framework, which quantifies how often and how severely operating limits are exceeded, creates an auditable methodology mixing equitable export allocation, distributed recourse, and AC risk assessment for active distribution network operators, and this framework is assessed using up to 1000 Monte Carlo scenarios per mixture and DOE design.

</details>

Abstract: Spline representations provide a compact way to approximate continuous trajectories using a finite number of parameters. When approximating trajectories generated by dynamical systems, the efficiency of a spline representation depends strongly on the placement of knot points. Uniform knot distributions often lead to inefficient representations, requiring either unnecessary control points or producing poor approximations in regions where the trajectory varies rapidly. In this work we propose a learning-based method for adaptive knot placement in B-spline approximations of trajectories generated by ordinary differential equations. A neural network learns a mapping from the initial condition of the system to quantities that characterize the local variation of the resulting trajectory. These estimates are used to construct nonuniform knot distributions that approximately equidistribute a local error indicator, reducing the number of control points needed to meet a prescribed accuracy.

</details>

Abstract: Automatic generation control (AGC) stabilizes frequency and scheduled power exchanges among interconnected areas, yet its reliance on communication networks introduces cyber-vulnerabilities. This work examines coordinated AGC cyber-attacks in the presence of remedial action schemes (RAS) and identifies operating conditions under which an attacker can drive grid frequency beyond safe limits while evading RAS. A feedback-based attack policy is used to manipulate AGC signals and steer system frequency toward unsafe trajectories. Then, a large nested-loop parametric sweep---spanning attacked-area inertia relative to its interconnected neighboring area, AGC capacity versus maximum tie-line transfer, AGC update rate, load damping, and bi-directional power transfers---generates over 1.45 million scenarios. These are classified using a decision tree to establish guidelines to distinguish successful from unsuccessful attacks based on frequency deviations. The resulting vulnerability guidelines are validated on a three-area system using phasor-domain simulations with IEEE 9-bus area models, detailed turbine--governor dynamics, and under/over-frequency load and generation shedding. Results show that specific combinations of AGC capacity, inertia distribution, and pre-attack tie-line loading increase the likelihood of successful attacks, offering operators actionable criteria for identifying vulnerable operating conditions.

</details>

Abstract: Changes in the generation mix of the U.S. Eastern Interconnection (EI) raise questions about long-term frequency performance, particularly inertia and primary frequency response (PFR). This paper jointly examines these characteristics using synchronized FNET/GridEye frequency measurements and confirmed generation-trip events from 2013 to 2025. A representative EI frequency trajectory is constructed using the median of geographically distributed measurements. Effective inertia is estimated quarterly from the early post-disturbance rate of change of frequency (RoCoF), using a 3-second window and least-squares fitting. PFR is evaluated using the beta metric, which measures aggregate frequency response from disturbance size and the A-to-B frequency deviation. Annual beta estimates are obtained after filtering events by starting frequency and disturbance size to improve comparability across years. Results show that EI effective inertia varies seasonally and follows a non-monotonic trend rather than a uniform long-term decline. Over the same period, coal generation decreased while natural gas, wind, and solar generation increased. Changes in synchronous generator commitment and operating conditions may have offset some expected inertia reductions. Filtered beta generally increased from the mid-2010s to the early 2020s, with year-to-year variability thereafter. The different trends show that early inertial behavior and broader aggregate frequency response have not evolved in parallel. Joint analysis of inertia and beta offers a more complete measurement-based assessment of EI frequency performance during the generation transition.

</details>

Abstract: Objectives: High-Speed Videoendoscopy glottal area waveforms conflate lateral fold excursion with longitudinal opening, precluding direct estimation of velocity, acceleration, and left-right asymmetry. Displacement waveforms preserve these quantities but are susceptible to spatial quantization and temporal sampling errors. We characterize these errors, evaluate reduction via Kalman smoothing and temporal interpolation, and determine optimal frame rate bounds relative to pixel excursion, speed quotient (SQ), and open quotient (OQ). Methods: Sampling theory was used to derive analytical frame-rate bounds required for kinematic feature fidelity. Kalman smoothing was compared with FFT-based denoising, paired with sinc or spline interpolation. Interpolation was evaluated on 4000 fps recordings (N=55) downsampled and reconstructed. A 16000 fps dataset (N=12) served as reference for noise reduction across 2000-8000 fps downsampled waveforms evaluated on peak displacement, velocity, OQ, and SQ. Results: Sinc interpolation yielded lower median mean absolute error than spline (0.29 vs 0.52 pixels), with 85% of recordings (47/55) versus 45% (25/55) below 0.5 pixels. For kinematic feature estimation (N=12), Kalman-sinc outperformed FFT-sinc in 75% of feature-by-frame-rate pairings. Conclusion: Computing kinematic features without artifacts requires joint consideration of temporal and spatial resolution. Kalman-sinc preprocessing at 8000 fps is recommended for velocity and SQ measures. Peak lateral displacement can be measured at 2000 fps, while OQ requires 4000 fps for typical spatial resolutions (9-14 pixels maximum excursion). Pixel excursion should be reported alongside kinematic measures to contextualize data reliability.

</details>

Abstract: Most deep learning systems for diabetic retinopathy (DR) grading are evaluated on a single data source, with limited evidence of cross-site generalisation, uncertainty calibration, and interpretability. We propose CalibCause, a quality-gated mixture-of-experts framework that combines per-expert visual explanations, label-free image-quality conditioning, knowledge distillation, and calibrated confidence with deferral. We evaluated the framework using 51,310 fundus images from EyePACS, DDR, and APTOS 2019, with IDRiD and DeepDRiD for external testing. Evaluation included pooled testing, leave-one-environment-out experiments, paired bootstrap confidence intervals, and local grade-threshold adaptation. On the pooled test split, the label-free distilled student achieved quadratic weighted kappa (QWK) of 0.863 and accuracy of 0.865 with 27.8 million parameters, 52% fewer than its teacher. Across held-out environments, the label-free model achieved mean QWK of 0.741 compared with 0.743 for a plain ConvNeXt-Tiny baseline, while adversarial invariance did not improve cross-site grading accuracy. Refitting grade thresholds using 200 labelled images increased held-out EyePACS QWK from 0.533 to 0.621 for the label-free model. After vector scaling, expected calibration error ranged from 0.025 to 0.036 across CalibCause variants, and deferring the least confident 20% of images increased student accuracy from 0.865 to 0.923 on the remaining images. These findings demonstrate that quality-aware grading, interpretable expert routing, model compression, and calibrated deferral can be integrated without sacrificing cross-site performance relative to a plain baseline, although reliable deployment still requires local validation and adaptation.

</details>

Abstract: Airway segmentation from chest CT enables branch-level measurement of airway size and growth, but peripheral bronchi remain hard to segment, particularly in children, whose airways are smaller and for whom few annotated scans exist. Different models recover complementary branches of the airway tree. Their union improves overall tree coverage but also introduces disconnected non-airway objects. We propose anchored fusion (A-Fuse), which takes the voxel-wise union of TfeNet, a retrained MEDPSeg and a reimplemented AirMorph and retains the connected component with greatest overlap with the AirMorph mask. On 30 held-out adult CTs from the ATM'26 training set, A-Fuse reaches a Dice score of 93.24% and the highest branch detection rate among compared methods (92.05%). On nine pediatric scans, it reaches 94.15% Dice and 68.79% branch detection. A-Fuse retains the airway tree in all 39 test scans, whereas keeping the largest component selects a non-airway object in four. A-Fuse is deployed as an automated web application and we release all code, trained weights and an open-source ITK-SNAP extension for 3D tubular segmentation editing.

</details>

Abstract: Despite their scale, large medical imaging datasets may underrepresent clinically relevant pathological presentations. Synthetic data can expand coverage, but findings must remain compatible with surrounding anatomy. This is challenging in ultrasound, where findings resemble surrounding tissue and must be positioned relative to anatomical landmarks. We propose DiffUltra, a task-guided latent diffusion pipeline that models pathological structure and spatial context, and screens synthetic images for downstream utility. DiffUltra conditions generation on structural masks decomposing findings into anechoic, hypoechoic, and hyperechoic subregions. Two placement strategies insert findings into pathology-absent backgrounds or replace existing findings with structurally matched pathology from other patients. A filter trained exclusively on real data retains images whose predictions agree spatially with target boxes. We evaluate DiffUltra for lung ultrasound detection of pleural effusion and consolidation, using over 700,000 expert-annotated frames from 598 patients at 12 sites. Averaged across three real-data scales, synthetic insertion significantly improved video-level AUROC for both pleural effusion (p = 0.017) and consolidation (p < 0.01). For pleural effusion, frame-level average precision improved by 4.5 percentage points on average and by 6.1 points using only 10% of real data (p < 0.01 for both). Synthetic insertion also yielded larger average precision gains than an existing diffusion-based lesion-insertion method at every scale. At full scale, targeted synthesis improved AUROC for both pathologies by 1.1-3.9 points in an underrepresented anatomical zone and on a held-out acquisition system. These results suggest that anatomically constrained, task-guided synthesis can help address coverage gaps in ultrasound training data.

</details>

Abstract: High-fidelity medical image synthesis is promising for data-efficient learning and privacy-preserving model development. However, most existing approaches rely on symbolic or label-based conditioning, which constrains generative diversity and limits case-specific contextual fidelity. Inspired by the Ship of Theseus paradox, we propose TheseusSyn, a novel context-conditioned synthesis paradigm that generates images through iterative content replacement. Our framework employs a multi-codebook discrete tokenizer to convert medical images into sequences of token indices, extracted with a global representation token that serves as a contextual constraint. A masked autoregressive generative model then iteratively masks and predicts tokens conditioned on both local neighborhoods and the global context. By progressively replacing image content until the entire token sequence is renewed, TheseusSyn produces synthetic samples that preserve anatomical coherence and semantic consistency while introducing controlled diversity. We evaluate the proposed framework on multiple datasets from MedMNIST by constructing synthetic datasets with varying masking strategies and training ResNet-18 classifiers exclusively on either real or synthetic data. Experimental results demonstrate that classifiers trained on TheseusSyn-generated images achieve competitive performance on official test sets, indicating that the synthesized data preserve diagnostically relevant features and semantic consistency. Systematic analysis of masking strategies reveals their impact on structural fidelity and downstream classification accuracy. These results suggest that context-conditioned masked autoregression provides an effective and generalizable solution for label-free medical image synthesis, offering a scalable alternative to conventional data augmentation and class-conditioned generative models in medical imaging applications.

</details>

Abstract: In the RSNA 2026 Knee Abnormality Detection challenge, 58 of 4,407 training studies have expert labels for 12 findings, and the rest have only a radiology report. We compare training targets built from a language-model reading of the reports and from a public image model against two references: the expert labels and the 39,248 study-finding cells that the reports state as present or absent. The report labels and the out-of-fold predictions of the image model reach similar agreement with the expert labels (macro AUC 0.892 and 0.896) but fail on different findings. The report is better for ligament tears and patellofemoral osteoarthritis, which it never calls present when the experts do not; the image model is better for effusion, Baker's cyst, contusion, and synovitis, where the reports call the finding absent, leave it out, or call it present more often than the experts. Their equal mixture reaches 0.927 (gain over the report labels 0.035, 95% interval 0.019 to 0.052), also after removing the studies whose knee side was handled inconsistently. We did not observe a model-level gain from this better target: a 2.5D ResNet50 trained on the report labels alone scored 0.877 on the private leaderboard, after the deadline, against 0.872 for the same model trained on the mixture, and the out-of-fold advantage of the mixture is confounded by a change of code and by possible leakage through the image model's folds. A scanner prevalence prior raised agreement with the reports but not with the experts, so a report-derived reference can reward reporting habits. A rank correction for co-occurring findings gave small out-of-fold gains and no visible leaderboard effect. Our entry, a forked public ensemble with that correction, was displayed at rank 582 of 6,299 on the private leaderboard (0.943, a score shared by 287 teams). All results are exploratory; none of the models is meant for clinical use.

</details>

Abstract: Automated fetal head circumference (HC) measurement reaches near-expert accuracy on the HC18 benchmark, but published models are trained and evaluated on hospital-grade equipment. We quantify what happens when such a model is carried to a low-cost handheld probe. A lightweight segmentation network (CSM, 0.13M parameters) was trained on HC18 across five independent runs and applied without modification to images from a $1,350 handheld probe (SonoQue C3), acquired at the same visit as the reference hospital scan in 18 patients. In-domain accuracy was 2.34 +/- 0.43 mm; on handheld images the same model gave 16.12 mm against the hospital record, a 6.9-fold degradation. Input preprocessing, a choice rarely reported, accounted for about a third of that gap: direct resizing gave 49.45 mm against 16.12 mm for an aspect-preserving pipeline. A leave-one-out data-efficiency curve showed that fine-tuning on two images significantly worsened accuracy, and that from four images upward error fell by 0.75 mm per doubling of the local dataset (p = 0.0045), although no training-set size up to 27 images significantly improved on the unmodified model. Much of the residual gap belongs to the reference rather than the model: scored against a manual tracing of the same image the model was already within 10.48 mm, and its disagreement with sonographer calipers on the same frame was a near-constant offset, negative in 10 of 10 cases. A single calibration constant estimated leave-one-out reduced that disagreement from 8.12 mm to 3.49 mm, reaching the intraobserver reproducibility reported for experienced sonographers, but did not improve agreement with the hospital record. Cross-device evaluations should therefore report the input preprocessing used and characterise the precision of their reference standard.

### [316] [CS-LDM: Clinically Guided Structure-Aware Latent Diffusion for Knee Radiographic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40717)

Abstract: Knee osteoarthritis (KOA) is a leading cause of physical disability and currently has no curative clinical treatment. Early diagnosis and accurate prognosis are therefore vital for managing its progression. Existing methods mainly predict future severity grades and provide limited visual explanation of structural change. Recent generation-guided approaches synthesize follow-up radiographs for progression assessment, but generated images are often judged by predictive accuracy or image realism, so they may satisfy a grading model while remaining morphologically unreliable. We therefore propose CS-LDM, a clinically guided, structure-aware latent diffusion framework that forecasts patient-specific future knee radiographs from the current X-ray and clinically available current KL grade, using LoRA adaptation, current-grade conditioning, and frozen-teacher diagnostic distillation. Experiments on the public Osteoarthritis Initiative (OAI) dataset show that CS-LDM improves future severity-grade consistency while maintaining competitive image fidelity. Further ordinal, OARSI, ROI-level, and stable/progressed analyses show that radiographic forecasting should be evaluated by balancing diagnostic consistency, anatomical plausibility, and structural preservation.

</details>

Abstract: Accurate clinically significant prostate cancer (csPCa) detection from prostate MRI requires a detailed understanding of the prostate anatomical context. Deep learning approaches have achieved promising results in zonal and lesion segmentation; however, the joint analysis of prostate zonal anatomy and lesion morphology in a unified framework remains relatively underexplored. In this work, we propose ZH-Mamba, a multi-stage zonal-aware csPCa detection framework consisting of two H-Mamba UNets models for prostate zonal segmentation and lesion localization, followed by an anatomy-aware Convolutional Neural Network classifier for csPCa detection. The proposed model leverages a learnable Hadamard Transform-based fusion mechanism to enhance zonal and lesion feature representations without increasing computational complexity. The learned fusion patterns consistently emphasize prostate regions, suggesting that the proposed fusion captures anatomically relevant feature representations. We demonstrate a strong zonal segmentation performance on the PI-CAI dataset, reaching Dice scores of $\textbf{0.95}$, $\textbf{0.85}$, and $\textbf{0.95}$ for whole gland (WG), peripheral zone (PZ), and transition zone (TZ), respectively, providing robust anatomical priors for downstream csPCa detection. Experimental results on an independent internal dataset confirm the generalization capability of the proposed zonal segmentation model. For lesion localization, the H-Mamba UNet achieves a competitive Dice score of $\textbf{0.70}$. Integrating the zonal masks and lesion localization, the proposed ZH-Mamba framework achieves an AUC of $\textbf{0.86}$, Average Precision ($\mathrm{AP}_{\mathrm{Patient}}$) of $\textbf{0.72}$, and F1 score of $\textbf{0.76}$, demonstrating the effectiveness of zonal-aware csPCa detection.

</details>


### [318] [LAPIS: Clinically Constrained Patient-Identity Residual Learning for Chest X-Ray Anonymization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40722)

Abstract: Biometric information in chest radiographs limits their release for research, as patients can be re-identified. We propose LAPIS, which jointly adapts a spatial chest-radiograph latent space so that patient-discriminative information concentrates in a compact, replaceable channel residual while most clinically relevant content stays in the complement. The residual is defined by an exactly semi-orthogonal basis, a spatial adversary suppresses identity leakage from the complement, and replacement-aware training teaches the decoder cross-patient residual swaps under output identity, clinical and realism constraints, so that replacing the residual yields a valid image. At release, the residual is replaced by a mixture of donor residuals. On patient-disjoint model, release and test sets from ChestX-ray14, we evaluate attackers adapted to the anonymized setting, a structural source-retrieval attack, and pathology classifiers trained on released images and tested on real ones. LAPIS reduces worst-case cross-study linkability from 0.961 to 0.791 AUC at a macro AUROC cost of 0.012 relative to reconstruction; a deformation-based baseline, however, remains better at structural source retrieval.

</details>
