Abstract: Dynamic semantic maps have become an important intermediate representation for many computer vision applications. Efficient lossless compression of such maps is essential for reducing storage and transmission costs. Existing contour-based semantic map compression methods mainly exploit spatial redundancy within individual frames while largely ignoring the strong temporal correlation between adjacent semantic maps. In this paper, we propose an inter-frame predictive contour coding framework for lossless semantic map compression. The proposed framework first predicts blob positions and label indexes from the reference frame and then performs contour prediction using corresponding chain-code units in the reference contours. A mode selection mechanism is further employed to choose between intra and inter coding according to the estimated coding cost. By exploiting temporal contour redundancy while preserving the advantages of contour-based representation, the proposed framework significantly improves coding efficiency. Experimental results on the VSPW dynamic semantic segmentation dataset demonstrate that the proposed method consistently outperforms state-of-the-art lossless semantic map compression methods. Compared with the recent context-adaptive extended chain coding framework, the proposed inter-frame coding achieves an average bitrate reduction of 3.0\% without significant increase in the average encoding time, demonstrating an effective trade-off between compression performance and computational complexity. The source code is made publicly available at https://github.com/InterDigitalInc/LosslessSegmentationMapCompression.

</details>

Abstract: Burst super-resolution (BSR) achieves higher reconstruction fidelity than single-image super-resolution (SISR) by aggregating sub-pixel information from multiple frames. However, it requires a burst of frames captured under near-static conditions, and such frames are unavailable for single photographs or archival images. We propose Pseudo-Burst Super-Resolution (PBSR), which brings BSR-style reconstruction to the single-image input setting by synthesizing pseudo-burst frames via zero-shot novel view synthesis (NVS). PBSR first generates pseudo-burst frames with realistic parallax using a viewpoint sampling strategy calibrated to the pixel-shift distribution of real burst datasets. The frames are then processed by a BSR backbone equipped with a geometry-aware alignment module that reuses the NVS camera parameters and estimated depth. While the NVS stage is zero-shot, the backbone is fine-tuned with an artifact-aware adaptation so that it can exploit the synthesized frames despite their generative artifacts. Experiments on the BurstSR dataset show that PBSR consistently improves in PSNR, SSIM, and LPIPS over the single-frame baseline with the same reconstruction backbone.

</details>

Abstract: Whole-slide image classifiers are usually developed within one hospital and often go untested at unseen hospitals. We assembled 5,036 haematoxylin-and-eosin-stained gastric slides from five Korean hospitals, scanned at 20x and 40x, and compared multiple-instance learning aggregators under leave-one-hospital-out validation on frozen pathology foundation-model features. A hierarchical patch-region-slide graph aggregator (CHIME-MIL) with site alignment and two-scale fusion reached a mean area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of 0.837, above six conventional attention and transformer aggregators trained without these steps. Under a matched recipe it did not exceed attention-based pooling, and global max pooling given the same alignment and fusion was better (AUC 0.855, P = 0.0002). The alignment, which subtracts each hospital's mean feature vector without labels, onboarded a new hospital from ten unlabelled slides and cost 0.002 AUC without held-out-hospital data, but its accuracy gains were small and inconsistent. The model had low top-label calibration error (0.053) but under-confident malignancy probabilities, and removing its most-attended patches lowered the malignancy probability far more than removing random patches. With these features, more complex aggregators did not improve discrimination; what a deployable system adds is label-free onboarding, calibration checks and auditable evidence.

</details>

Abstract: Image reconstruction for positron emission tomography (PET) is a challenging inverse problem due to its inherent ill-posedness and Poisson counting noise, particularly in low-dose acquisitions. In this work, we introduce a novel Bayesian PET reconstruction framework that leverages a flow-matching generative prior learned from high-quality PET images. By incorporating this prior, we formulate PET reconstruction as a Bayesian inference problem and solve it via latent-space posterior sampling using the unadjusted Langevin algorithm. We evaluate the proposed method through numerical experiments and benchmark it against classical reconstruction methods and state-of-the-art deep learning-based baselines. The proposed method achieves improved reconstruction performance while enabling posterior uncertainty quantification.

</details>


### [323] [PRIN: A Perceptual-Structural Retinex-Inspired Network for Low-Light Image Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41338)
*Qiyi Xie,Wei Chen Ng,Ziyao Li,Yuning Wu,Huasen Wu,Tianshi Miao,Jian Ding Tan*

Abstract: Low-light image enhancement (LLIE) focuses not only on visibility recovery but also on preserving structurally meaningful content and perceptual naturalness. Existing learning-based methods may achieve favorable pixel-wise fidelity while still producing over-smoothed textures, color deviations, or spatially incoherent illumination. Retinex-inspired methods provide interpretable decomposition into reflectance and illumination components, however, the asymmetric restoration roles of these components are rarely encoded through branch-specific frequency-domain refinement. To address this issue, we propose a Perceptual-Structural Retinex-Inspired Network (PRIN), which integrates Retinex priors with restoration-role-aware enhancement. PRIN employs a shared encoder and asymmetric decoders to estimate reflectance and illumination components. The reflectance branch performs attention-guided and edge-aware wavelet refinement to preserve textures and boundaries, whereas the illumination branch promotes spatially coherent brightness adjustment. After the recomposition of reflectance and illumination branches, a curve-based post-composition enhancement head calibrates residual tone and exposure deviations. Experiments on LOL-v1, LOL-v2-Syn, and LOL-v2-Real show that the proposed network ties for the best structural similarity index (SSIM) and obtains the second-lowest learned perceptual image patch similarity (LPIPS) on LOL-v1, achieves the best SSIM and LPIPS on LOL-v2-Syn, and obtains the highest peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) and the second-lowest LPIPS on LOL-v2-Real.

</details>

Abstract: We developed TopoHyperClinicalGraph, a multimodal framework combining hyperbolic tissue geometry, persistent topology and clinical graph learning for cancer survival prediction. Pre-extracted UNI2-h features are reduced from 1,536 to 1,024 dimensions using training-fold PCA. A pathology backbone combines hyperbolic neighborhoods with Lorentz-centroid attention pooling. Once frozen, its survival logits are adjusted using persistence descriptors from up to 64 landmarks per slide and sampling controls. A five-node clinical graph then learns a further correction from age, sex, stage, T and N.
  Five-fold patient-level evaluation covered eight TCGA and CPTAC cohorts with 2,364 patients. Mean validation-selected C-indices for TopoHyperClinicalGraph versus pathology-only spatial PatchGCN were 0.7563 versus 0.7386 in COADREAD, 0.6220 versus 0.5998 in HNSC, 0.6755 versus 0.6535 in BRCA and 0.6409 versus 0.6176 in STAD. CPTAC scores were 0.5984 in HNSC, 0.6060 in PDA, 0.8055 in CCRCC and 0.7192 in LUAD. CPTAC-HNSC used TCGA-initialized adaptation; other CPTAC backbones were trained within their cohorts. Geometry accounted for most pathology-only gains, with smaller persistence effects. Clinical graph fusion exceeded its matched node-wise control in five cohorts, tied in one and scored lower in two; clinical-only prediction exceeded fusion in three CPTAC cohorts.
  The framework supports separate evaluation of geometric, topological and clinical contributions. All scores were validation-selected; independent testing is needed to establish generalizability.

</details>

Abstract: This work introduces three lightweight enhancement modules for medical lesion segmentation to improve clinical applicability, particularly under data-scarce and computationally constrained environments. Current mainstream methods struggle with the lack of high-quality annotations for rare pathologies, which often leads to severe morphological distortions during inference, despite achieving seemingly ideal DSC scores. Furthermore, the isolated development of individual segmentation tasks restricts cross-domain methodological innovation, underscoring the need for leveraging shared knowledge across different lesions within the same anatomical region. However, constrained access to historical data and annotations hinders efficient knowledge extraction and triggers catastrophic forgetting when adapting to new classes. To address these challenges, we first propose DT-Morph Combined Loss with continuous spatial pooling to optimize differentiable topological structures and boundary continuity. Second, we introduce 3D Group Prompt, which extracts domain-universal knowledge via curated volumetric image-mask alignment prompts together with text prompts built on SAT baseline. Finally, we replace conventional output layers with Many-to-One Conditional Segmentation Head equipped with synonym-resolved routing, which brings negligible computational cost and enables fast incremental learning for new categories without sacrificing performance on old tasks. Ablation studies on abdominal and extra-abdominal datasets covering both organ and lesion segmentation verify the efficacy of our modules.

</details>


### [326] [IGDA-ICMH: an Importance Guided Feature and Network Dual Adaptation Framework towards Image Compression for Machine and Human Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41924)

Abstract: With the surge of machine vision applications, image compression for both machine and human vision (ICMH) has become an important research area. Existing studies mainly focus on task-specific adaptation or multi-task joint optimization, yet they often overlook the unequal contributions of different features and network layers to downstream machine vision tasks. To address this issue, we first quantitatively investigate the impact of different feature and network layers on machine vision performance, providing a principled understanding of their importance. Based on this analysis, an Importance Guided feature and network Dual Adaptation (IGDA) framework is developed for ICMH. It adopts implicit neural representation as the basic architecture and designs an efficient adaptation structure with both feature and network weight adaptation. An Importance-Guided Network Adaptation (IGNA) module is developed with a low-rank adaptation strategy to selectively adjust key network layers according to their importance in different tasks. An Importance-Guided Feature Adaptation (IGFA) module is developed with a low-rank and Kronecker product-based transform to explore the non-local property of the features in order to reduce the number of adaptation parameters. Extensive experiments on representative machine vision tasks, including image classification and object detection, demonstrate that our IGDA-ICMH framework achieves significantly better performance than the existing implicit neural representation-based methods for ICMH.

</details>
